# Batch Summary — Volume 04, Batch 0001 (Chapters 151–160)

> **Reading order in this file.** The **Volume 04, Batch 0001 delivery record** is at the head, below. Beneath it, unaltered and in this order, is the **Volume 03, Batch 0005 record** (the volume's climax and its open handoff), then the Batch 0004, Batch 0003, Batch 0002, Batch 0001 and Volume 03 Planning records. **A delivery record is added, not substituted.**

## Outcome

**Volume 04, Batch 0001 is complete. Chapters 151–160 exist in `chapters/volume-04/` as finished prose in close third person on Marek Venn, with no viewpoint shift anywhere in the batch, and the protagonist named in ten of ten.** It is the first batch of Volume 04 and it was written against `outline/volume-04.md`, which was written in the same phase, and against `outline/batches/volume-04-batch-0001.md`, whose ten cards were written in the writing session.

**The batch's spine, as delivered:** the market form is back on a board four days up the road with a different seal number on it and nobody put it up; a cart stands at a gate with a paper on it and the news came down the passage before the timber did; the guild's offer of the timber is a load with a person responsible for it, and this boundary will not fill in two lines; an eighth room is struck out of a printed Compact schedule in nine seconds from three steps up with no reason given; a carpenter comes down four days of road and is told *bring your keeper*; a man of forty-nine comes down four days of road with nothing in his hands in order to be asked for something, and he has not been asked in eleven years and found out this month that he still wanted to be; **a store is a room, and a room has a keeper, and a keeper can be asked in a room with the terms out loud, and a keeper can say no**; the joinwood for a brace is two wedges at a school-door head and two beech lengths out of a school's own store, given by the woman who keeps that door, who says in the same breath that the answer to a door with no wedge in it is a person and that there is not one available; **a brace is a physical support joined to a bearing mark and this building has one of the four things it needs, and the mark in the only stone in the building with a mark cut in it has been read three ways this month and has not said anybody its limit**; the air engineer asks the engineer for a work in the crew's own practice and says out loud before anybody can that the asking is the beginning of a standing; a route-holder's post comes up a passage with a carpenter's name in the second line and he does not refuse it and does not accept it and gives the reason, which is that a refusal given before a thing has been understood is a thing a man says to look clean; a physician asks a clerk to read a wall instead of reading it herself, a pump maintainer gives a figure nobody asked her for, and a schoolteacher writes a condition with no number and no day on it and is not met; the pot goes to nine days; and a man who keeps a store asks a room four words out loud and nobody answers it.

**Four things the batch refused, deliberately. No System panel of any kind** — no stat block, no item table, no quest text, no experience bar, no rank, and the correct amount of System in this batch is none. **No power stage** — the Bracer is Batch 0002 and is not started in this batch; the Marker is spent, the mark is off the wall, a carpenter said in a room he would not put it back, the Reader reading is not repeatable, the Chapter 99 claim's condition is unmet, the right hand is lost and the left hand does not close. **No mark cut in the stone** — the four marks and the groove on the lintel above the open school corner's mouth are read three ways by three people this month, the one undamaged thing on that stone is the one that was cut to be read, and it says nobody the limit. **And the eleven and the ten were not joined, the blank page and the low end were not joined, and the store's list and the room count were joined deliberately, by a schoolteacher, in her own words, in a clerk's book, with a reason.**

## The calendar this batch is locked to

**The week runs Monday to Sunday. 151–155 the thirtieth week; 156–160 the thirty-first.** 151 the first day, a Monday; 152 the second, a Tuesday; 153 the third, a Wednesday; 154 the fourth, a Thursday; 155 the fifth, a Friday; 156 the first day of the thirty-first week, a Monday; 157 the second, a Tuesday; 158 the third, a Wednesday; 159 the fourth, a Thursday; 160 the fifth, a Friday. **The corridor is shut on the first, second and fourth days and open on the third, fifth and sixth, and the seventh day is not a crossing day; the school runs on the three open days.** So the corridor is **shut in 151, 152, 154, 156, 157 and 159** and **open in 153, 155, 158 and 160**, and the **school ran in 153, 155, 158 and 160 — the twenty-third, twenty-fourth, twenty-fifth and twenty-sixth mornings — all four in the room at the kitchen-line end.** The school corner is open and is not used and no chapter in this batch put the school back in it. **The market day is the sixth day of a week and is not in this batch**, and the sixth day of the twenty-ninth week is behind it. **The spoken measure of elapsed weeks is a separate register**, is not re-derived, is not corrected and dates nothing; from the thirtieth week onward *thirty* is the week clock itself. Counts that track the week clock are re-derivable and monotonic in this batch: the review page at the low end has been blank since the fifteenth week, the plan has been transcribing the anteroom's count since the fifteenth week, the clerk has been this boundary's clerk since the nineteenth week, and the anteroom frame's packing went in on the third day of the eighteenth week. **No day number is attached to any in-batch event; the day-number register's highest value remains the forty-sixth day; the only date on any object is the date in the top corner of the plan.** **Wen Pask's count is 86, 87, 88, 89, 90, 91, 92, 93, 94 and 95 across the ten chapters, one a day, all ten on the page in the right chapter**, and it is not a register and it dates nothing. **No forward count of days or weeks on a courier**: the road is four days up the middle passage to the middle gate, no figure is attached to the second gate, and a sheet came back on a board by a schedule with nothing on it saying when.

## Movement

- **Chapter 151 — The Sheet Is Back On The Board. Thirtieth week, first day, a Monday, the corridor shut, the school did not run.** A road hand with a wooden tally comes down the passage and says what she is before she is asked, as she has for thirty weeks, and brings two things: **the market form is back on the board at the gate four days up the road with a seal number on it that is not the number it had before, and nobody put it up**; and **there is a cart at the middle gate with a paper on it, and she did not see the timber and is not going up a road to look at a cart.** A hundred copies of a halfpenny sheet is not a delivery, and the three Hearthholds that took it now have timber and can shut a route when a clerk at a counter says so. **Talla Roke says the sentence the whole month turns on: a form and a load are the same instrument, and the moment that cart is moving this Hearthhold is a place something is being taken out of by a form, and she names the bundle in the anteroom and says it is not hers to make into a speech.** Marek Venn says the answer to the clerk's question at the seventh bell is a piece of paper on a cart and that he does not like it, and says in a room that the price of a refusal is a wall in lime and the price of a purchase is a name on a paper, and that the person who keeps the schoolroom is not in the anteroom and is not to be asked about it in a room she is not standing in. Tams Quill refuses the ninth reason until Thursday. Board walked at sixteen. Count eighty-six.
- **Chapter 152 — Two Lines And A Load. Second day, a Tuesday, the corridor shut.** A carrier comes up on foot with a paper, because the cart is not coming, and puts it on the table and reads the outside of it first. **It is seven lines and two of them are for a name and for the person responsible for the load, and the word in it for not doing the carrying is *attended*, and Dalla Sen argues five of the seven out loud before anything is marked.** Bel Ordry says the half against it: the four lines about the load are the only part that would help anybody, and this Hearthhold has wanted to be able to say a thing went out of here for thirty weeks. **The eighth striking-out in this book happens in nine seconds from three steps up, with no reason given, and it is a printed Compact schedule with a column headed for the person who will be present and three rooms under it; Nera Oduya strikes the heading and the three rooms and says the word *column*.** A courier asks what the unstruck third room says and **a carrier answers it: it says the third room is one somebody is coming to on a day, and nobody has put anybody's name on it because there is not anybody to put, and that is what a schedule is for.** The packet is not refused in this chapter. **Marek Venn says the two available actions and then says the price of each out loud, in that order, and says he does not intend to say which one he would pay in a room either.** Count eighty-seven. Board not walked, by the clerk and by the count, for two different reasons.
- **Chapter 153 — A Store With A Keeper In It. Third day, a Wednesday, the corridor open, the school ran for the twenty-third morning.** **The fourth asking in thirty-one weeks**, in a room, with the terms out loud, Renn Vale named as the person who can be told no, **and the three who could not be asked named with a third set of reasons**: Leena Orr, because she is the person who will have to be asked whether the schoolroom's wall can take a push; Pell Roke, because he reads numbers and a measurement a person has read aloud and nobody has written down is a figure in the column with nothing over the top of it; Tams Quill, because if he is also the hands then the man who records what a thing turned out to be is the man who found out. **Three hours, two of Iven Tallo's and one of Marek Venn's, and the carpenter names the split himself and gives the reason: a carpenter looking at a pile of beech is a different thing from an engineer looking at a pile of beech.** **The last of the terms is that nobody in this Hearthhold's name goes into that store's book.** Iven Tallo **gives back half of the sentence he struck out of a plan's back in the twenty-eight week: he was right about the word and wrong about one of the reasons, and the mark is not absent, it is in the lintel over the school corner's mouth, and four hundred years of damage and nobody has got it.** Marek Venn says out loud, unasked, that **the limit on a brace will not be a figure of his in a room**, because the mark is damaged and he cannot read it, **and the limit will be a condition written by the person the brace can hurt.** Two pairs of hands go four days up the passage and come back with the fact that **the keeper said no to the timber and said *bring your keeper*.** **Crew's column moves by three, to sixty-six and a half.** Count eighty-eight.
- **Chapter 154 — The Reaction Goes On The Schoolroom Wall. Fourth day, a Thursday, the corridor shut, the school did not run.** The water has been off the fill since the second day and nobody can say why, and **Nera Oduya says the part that is hers: the air at that end goes down the channel before the water does, so if the water is not doing it then the nine children are doing it, and she will not be the somebody who says that.** The cook says the half of it that is hers and asks for the children to be cold in the record. **A man reads four sentences out of a survey and they are the whole of what a brace needs: a tested support, a counter-bearing, time to let the load settle, joined to a mark. This Hearthhold has the first and half of the second and no time and no mark.** **The counter-bearing is the chapter: the low end of the corridor is forbidden because a brace that reacts at the lowest point in a building is a brace that puts a person there, and the third condition on a blank page says that nobody is to be sent anywhere to make it true, and the clerk reads it out of her own book before he has finished saying it.** So it goes on the party wall, and **the survey camp's finding that the wall is *a bad repair on a sound wall* is what makes the brace possible and the same sheet says that a sound sector gets no crew put in it again, and Jo Verrick says those are the same sentence and she is tired of that page.** **The mark is not his and is not chalk: four marks and a groove, read two ways by two people, and a carpenter gives the mason's opinion and it is worth nothing and he gives it anyway, and the only undamaged mark on that stone is the one cut to be read.** **Tams Quill gives the ninth reason for refusing to head the column, and it is the first of the nine that is not about him: a heading is how a room finds out what the person in it is for, and there are rooms in this building that have never been asked that.** Board walked. Count eighty-nine.
- **Chapter 155 — What A Keeper Can Say No To. Fifth day, a Friday, the corridor open, the school ran for the twenty-fourth morning.** **Ormer Hatch, forty-nine, thirty-one years, survey camp, comes down the nine steps on his own with nothing in his hands, says what he is and what he keeps, and says he is not here to ask for anything.** He sent a carpenter down with a message that was his keeper's: *a store is a room and I keep it, I would like to hear the terms from whoever keeps whatever is asking for it.* **And he says the reason for the message: eleven years is a long time to be the reason a thing is in a place, and he wants to be asked.** **The two available actions are said out loud by the keeper of the page and the room finds a third one, and it is not clever: a store has a keeper, and a keeper can be asked with the terms out loud, and a keeper can say no.** The two who can be asked in a room on a landing say so. **The price is not money, because there is no money in this building, and it is two things: no name of his on any paper of this boundary's, and the whole of his list read out in a room with him in it, including the lines that are nearly out, and the fourth paragraph of a guild notice named in that room as the thing it is.** Bel Ordry refuses the paragraph for that morning and gives the reason and the terms. **The list has no date on it, and the reason is a schoolteacher's shape of a count, given by her in her own mouth without knowing she was giving it.** Talla Roke says the half against it — a thing given by a person and not carried by a person is a favour with a keeper — and then says the half against her own half. **Fourteen lines are read, twice, and the ninth is a dash and the dash is read twice because the man asked for it.** Count ninety. Board not walked.
- **Chapter 156 — The Air Engineer Asks The Engineer. Thirty-first week, first day, a Monday, the corridor shut by the arrangement, the school did not run and lost nothing.** **Volume 04's sixth act of trust, and it is the inverse of the third: the air engineer asks the engineer, in a room, with the terms out loud, using the crew's own practice, naming the three she is not going to ask and the reason for each before she asks him anything.** The work is to go and look at the air at the low end of the corridor, from above, not from inside it, and to come back and tell a room and not to tell her first. **She says the cost out loud before anybody can say it after her: the asking puts a standing on him, and a standing is the first half of a post, and she has thought about that and is doing it anyway and would like the word *standing* in the book and not the word *help*.** He says yes, and says three things about it, the third being that he wants to go. **The clerk decides which column the hours go in, in a room, and gives the reason out loud, and it is the same reason as in the twenty-eighth week — a man is not an hour — and the second reason is new: she is not the crew and has never once asked the five of them for anything, and this is what the boundary's column is for.** **The third volume question is answered by his own mouth and resolves nothing: he is going to go and look at the air on Wednesday, and after that he does not know, and the clerk enters the first half and not the second and enters that she has had a fortnight of wanting to enter the second.** **Her condition is not met and she says so and says that keeping itself and being asked to open a road while something is going wrong four days up a passage are not the same sentence, and she sets a second condition and says it out loud twice, and the reason for saying it twice is that the first is a sentence and the second is a fact, and nobody asks her in a room.** Count ninety-one. Board not walked.
- **Chapter 157 — A Post With A Name In A Register. Second day, a Tuesday, the corridor shut.** A carrier comes up with a stiff cover, a proper seal, and no date, and establishes the shape of his own patience in the clerk's book before handing it over. **The guild's fourth paragraph comes back for the fourth time and this time it pays: a route-holder's post, a bond, a wage in coin, hours entered against a named route in a register, and a boy back on the roll with a number on it and not *the apprentice of* anything.** Iven Tallo reads it out in full and does not refuse it and does not accept it, and gives the reason: **a refusal given before a thing has been understood is a thing a man says to look clean, and he has watched a man do it in this building, and it was him, in the twenty-second week, about the sill.** **Dalla Sen reads the document against itself and finds the hole, and the hole is not the guild's: the post is in this Hearthhold, and this boundary's own charter says in terms that no holder of a route here is to be entered by the office against any work here, and the guild knows it.** **Renn Vale says the sentence that makes the offer into what it is — it is a hand out of a gap in a clause wearing a bond and a wage and an apprentice's number, and a person in a bad month is going to hold the wage and not look at the hand — and says the person it will find is a boy on a bench, and a boy's roll number is a route, and everybody in the room knows it.** Bel Ordry says the half against Iven Tallo: the post is at a camp four and a half days up a road that took the halfpenny sheet, and that camp has timber, and a camp with timber can shut a route when a clerk at a counter says so, and has not had to yet. **Iven Tallo asks where the post is, is told, and says nine words: the first one out of a room with eleven people in it was a girl of nine.** The carrier writes a not-refusal on the outside of the stiff cover with no name on it. Count ninety-two. Board not walked by either, for the count's own reason.
- **Chapter 158 — Joinwood Out Of A Door. Third day, a Wednesday, the corridor open, the school ran for the twenty-fifth morning.** **The fire in the corner of that room has not been lit for ten weeks and the cook asks to be asked a sixth time and refuses a sixth time, in one sentence, and asks for the children to be cold in the record in the same place as the refusal.** Four men carry beech down the passage in pieces a cart could not have carried, and **every piece is entered in a clerk's book with no name against it and no seal and no form.** **The brace cannot be built and the chapter says why: joinwood is not timber, a wedge is not joinwood, there are no pegs and no bronze and no iron and a wooden pin in green beech will split in a year, and a carpenter says it in his own trade.** **Renn Vale gives the two wedges at the school-door head and the two beech lengths out of the school store, and says the half against it in the same breath: a door with no wedge is a door that has to be watched, and the answer is a person, and there is not a person available, and the page where a person would go is blank with three conditions on it and the third of them says nobody is to be sent anywhere to make it true, and she has better answers and has looked and has not found one.** **Pell Roke reads the fourteen lines of the store's list, and the numbers are not counts of anything, and the ninth line is a dash, and he said the dash is a thing a man does not promise and he is not going to argue with him because he reads numbers and that is the end of what he does.** **The store's list and what came down the passage agreed, line by line, and it is the first time in thirty-one weeks that a document in this building and a fact in a room have been the same thing.** Marek Venn says he does not know what to do with a gift that has a person in it. **The board is walked and a seventeenth thing goes on it — a store — in the column with nothing over the top of it, and the reason is a courier's rule arriving in a place it was not built for: a board that only holds things in one building cannot be read by a person in another one.** Count ninety-three.
- **Chapter 159 — The Wall That Takes The Push. Fourth day, a Thursday, the corridor shut, the school did not run.** A physician asks for a mark on a wall and asks for the clerk to read it and not her, and gives the reason for the first: **a filled ground bears differently than a drained one, and a wall that has had water standing against the bottom of it has been standing in its own wet, and when the wet goes the wall finds out what it weighs, and it finds that out by moving, in the direction it was already leaning.** **A line of chalk and a piece of string goes on the party wall, not cut and not a bearing mark, and Jo Verrick gives a number nobody asked her for — two inches and a bit in an hour — and says what it means and what it does not mean, and the clerk enters that it is the first figure anybody has taken at the waist on purpose in thirty-one weeks and that the three reasons for never taking one are not withdrawn and the figure is here anyway, and that the plan has no row for that wall and the refusal stands.** **The pot goes to nine days, said out loud in a room by the cook, with the reason that the seam gave one jar where it gives two and Jo has not measured it since the nineteenth week, and with the second reason that a dead man's share has been going back into that pot since the fourth day of the twenty-fifth week on purpose and is not going to be done for a brace.** **Mero Senn names the person in the brace and it is not a child: the woman who keeps that room, standing on a floor, nine times a week, with a cut in it made with stone and no bar, and a fire unlit for ten weeks, and a description in a clerk's book and not a name on a page.** He is asked for the number, gives it — four tons, or thereabouts, and the words *or thereabouts* go in the book next to it — **and the schoolteacher writes a condition with no number and no day on it, and it is about the brace and not about the room: the brace stands while the two of them still agree that the wall can take it, the first of them to stop agreeing takes the brace out, neither has to give a reason to the other first, and the second to find out is the one who acts.** **It is not met and the brace is not up.** Count ninety-four.
- **Chapter 160 — Who Owns A Wall. Fifth day, a Friday, the corridor open, the school ran for the twenty-sixth morning.** **Ormer Hatch comes up with a slate of his own and asks the volume's question out loud: who owns a wall.** He says before he asks it that he is not asking it as a complaint, and that the not-being-on-the-plan did not feel like a gift, it felt like a room with nothing in it about him, and that he has about a week of store left and two days to find out an answer he cannot find out. **Four people try to answer it and none of them does.** Bel Ordry says a page in a coat is not a system and that neither he nor the page owns a wall, and then says the half against his own answer: a thing nobody owns is maintained by whoever is standing near it, and that is not keeping it. Talla Roke says the only person who knows is a man who has never been the keeper of a wall. **Nera Oduya does not have half of it and says so, and says she would give it if she had, and asks for that to be entered so that a person in a year does not think there was a woman here who knew and would not say.** **Marek Venn says he knows the answer for two of the four and refuses to say it twice in a room, and gives the reason: saying it in a room is making a claim, and he is not going to be the first man in this building to find a loophole in a thing thirty people agreed to.** **The clerk is asked to put two questions next to each other for the third time in a month and refuses for the third time and enters the three refusals in one sentence with the two questions.** Nobody answers. Hatch says that was all right, and that his answer to how a thing gets looked after is *by somebody who is standing near it who can be asked*, and asks for it read back to him. **The count is read at ninety-five and is not going to be ninety-six, the board is walked at seventeen entries, and the column is unheaded for the ninth batch.**

## Required character, relationship and power change

- **Power: nothing advanced. The correct amount of System in this batch is no panel of any kind.** The Bracer is Batch 0002 and is not built here. The four sentences that define it are read out loud in a room in Chapter 154 and three of the four things it needs are in this Hearthhold and the fourth is not. **A room became usable in this batch because a carrier read his own paper aloud, a woman struck a column out of a schedule in nine seconds, a clerk argued five of seven lines before a mark was made, a woman with a rule gave away two wedges and said what they cost, a man said *bring your keeper* and a keeper came four days and was asked, a boy read fourteen lines of a stranger's inventory out loud, a pump maintainer gave a figure nobody asked for, a physician asked a clerk to read a wall instead of herself, and a schoolteacher wrote a condition about a brace instead of about her room.** The plan did not decide where anybody went and opened nothing. The right hand's fine control is not recovered, the left hand does not close, the brass rule is silent for longer than twenty-two weeks, the Reader claim's condition is unmet, the four marks and the groove answer nothing, and no mark was cut in the stone anywhere in this building.
- **Nera Oduya: the sixth of the volume's acts of trust, and it is the inverse of the third.** **She asks him for a work**, in a room, with the terms out loud, in the crew's own practice, with a person named who can be told no and the three not asked named with the reason for each. **She says the cost out loud before anybody can say it after her: the asking is the first half of a post.** She is asked for a piece of work and not consulted for a decision. **In Chapter 160 she is the only person in the book who does *not* have half the answer, and she says so and offers it anyway**, which varies the third volume's pattern and is the reason the fourth is not a repeat of the third. **She does not say her theory, her condition is approached and is not met, she sets a second condition and says it out loud twice, and nobody asks her in a room.** She is not leaving and no chapter stages her going. **No intimacy, no recognition scene, no acceleration, and no scene whose only content is the two of them.**
- **The working practice holds in every record, and the batch adds its material form.** The person who keeps a room writes the change to it: four conditions on four rows in Volume 03, a corridor's terms in Volume 03, a limit stated in Volume 03, **and in this batch a condition on a brace written by the woman who keeps the wall the brace can hurt, with two people in it and no number and no day on it; two lines struck out of a stranger's form before a mark was made; a title's shape argued in a room; a post's hole found by a clerk reading a document against itself; a store's list read twice and compared.** **And the batch's addition is that a store is a room, a room has a keeper, and a keeper can be asked with the terms out loud and can say no — which is the same practice pointed at material instead of at people, and it is the volume's civic discovery and it is about a pile of timber.**
- **Iven Tallo is not redeemed, not a defector, not a martyr and not a saint, and nothing in this batch made him any of those four things.** He is asked a fourth time and the four hours are his and one other person's, on a named asking with the terms out loud. **He gives back half of a sentence he wrote in the twenty-eighth week and says he was right about the word and wrong about one of the reasons, and a carpenter's mark is a masman's opinion worth nothing that he gives anyway.** He is offered a post with his name in a register, in coin, with a boy back on the roll, **and he does not refuse it and does not accept it, and gives the reason, and asks for the entry to say the date he refuses is not today.** Nothing in this batch may make him any of those four things and nothing did.
- **Bel Ordry:** a page in a coat is not a system and he is still the only person who has to say it; **he turned the volume's argument on its own ground by refusing to name the guild's fourth paragraph in a room on a Friday morning to persuade a man with eleven years of beech to part with it, and said the reason and set a price that was not haggle on the parchment**; and he said the half of the answer that was against his own answer.
- **Dalla Sen:** named the three who could not be asked before asking anybody and said the third set of reasons; **decided which column a man's hours go in, in a room, and gave the reason out loud twice; read a document against itself and found that the hole was not the guild's; argued five of seven lines out loud before a mark was made; entered the days the count has been read and nobody has thanked her and that she is glad; refused three times in a month to put two questions next to each other and entered the three refusals in one sentence; and entered that she had had a fortnight of wanting to enter a second half of an entry and had not.** She never walks the board and the count never walks the board in the same chapter, and each of them gives a reason.
- **Tams Quill:** the crew's column moved by three on a named asking; **he gave the ninth reason for refusing to head the column with nothing over the top of it, and it is the first of the nine that is not about him — a heading is how a room finds out what the person in it is for, and there are rooms in this building that have never been asked that**; he refused to spend a reason before the middle of the week; he entered the seventeenth board entry himself and gave the reason for putting a store in the unheaded column; he refused a tenth reason; and he is in the batch's closing image as the only person in this building whom the clerk has never once asked.
- **Renn Vale:** gave two wedges and two beech lengths out of a school store and said the half against it in the same breath, and the half against it is the volume's argument in one sentence: a door with no wedge is a door that has to be watched, the answer is a person, and there is not a person available, and the page where a person would go is blank with three conditions on it. She said the sentence that makes the guild's post into what it is. She said the half of the answer that was against the man giving it. She said in Chapter 154 that she is going to have to decide something about a wall and has not practised it.
- **Ilsa Norr:** asked to be asked about the fire and refused a sixth time; **gave the reason a store's list has no date on it, in her own mouth, and did not know she was giving it, and it is the shape of a count she has been teaching a room since the twenty-ninth week**; **asked a physician for the number a brace will push with, and said she would rather be argued with than talked out of it by a small number; and wrote a condition about a brace and not about her room, and said the cost of it out loud, and said that she is not going to be able to stand in that doorway and explain the difference between frightened and wrong.**
- **Mero Senn:** asked for a mark on a wall and asked for the clerk to read it and not himself; **named the person in the brace and refused to name a child and said why; gave four tons, or thereabouts, and made the clerk write the words *or thereabouts* next to the four tons; and said the number is not an argument, it is an instrument he has to carry into a room every morning and read out loud.** He is not a saint and is not a rebuke and is right.
- **Pell Roke:** read the fourteen lines of a stranger's inventory out loud, including the ninth one twice, and said the numbers are not counts of anything and the dash is a thing a man does not promise, **and said he was not going to argue with the man about it because he reads numbers and that is the end of what he does.** Named as one of the three who could not be asked, with a new reason. He did not go down the steps, into the corridor, or over the waist.
- **Wen Pask:** paid, kept, **ninety-five days and ninety-five nights at the end of the batch, and dead.** His count is read at a sixth bell in **all ten chapters**, is not going to be ninety-six, and **the reading is not made into a scene about grief, because the reading is a working practice and not a funeral.** His count is in the column with nothing over the top of it and in nothing else. **His bundle is in the anteroom and it is named out loud in a room for the first time in the book, by a courier, who says it is not hers to make into a speech.**
- **Ormer Hatch: new in this batch.** Forty-nine, thirty-one years, survey camp, keeper of a store. **He is not an antagonist, not a saint and not a lesson.** He camped the timber four days up the middle passage and said he would do it again. He said no to the timber and said *bring your keeper*. He came four days with nothing in his hands in order to be asked. He named a fourth paragraph nobody had named in a room. He asked the volume's question out loud, and nobody answered it, and he said that was all right.
- **The woman from the wash rooms:** not named, not asked, not rostered, not sent for. **A printed Compact schedule with a column headed for the person who will be present and three rooms under it was struck through in nine seconds from three steps up, before anybody had asked, and the reason was not given, and nobody asked.** The floor was not approached, nobody went near it, nobody was sent, and the page at the low end is blank and has been for thirty-one weeks.
- **Oren Sill is not named. Dael Harrow is not named. Cassian Rook does not appear. The One-Seat is not named. Leena Orr's *a list is not a location* was not used again, and that is now four batches held. A Quiet Hands cell is unchanged and uncaught, and nothing in this batch recruits anybody, turns their reason into a riddle or a voice, or restates it. No new final enemy.**

## Material and labour hand-off

- **The market route.** The form is **back on the board** four days up the middle passage, with a seal number on it that is not the number it had before, and **nobody put it up**, and it came back by a schedule. **The board is still standing and the seal is still on the road.** A competent person at that counter told a courier in the twenty-ninth week that a sheet comes back on a board in a month by a clerk who does not know about Tuesday, and **this batch did not repeat that discovery and did not correct it.**
- **The load.** A cart is standing at the middle gate with a paper on it and a carrier up the passage with a copy. **A guild load's paper with seven lines and two of them for a person has been read out in an anteroom, five of the seven argued out loud before anything was marked, and refused. The cart is not coming up the middle passage and nobody in this building has sent anybody or anything.** **The boundary's decision not to fill in two lines is unchanged and is not re-argued in this batch, and it has a price, and the price has been said out loud in a room by the man who recommended the refusal and by the cook, and the two of them named the same thing, which is a bundle in an anteroom and a wall in lime that is not being built.**
- **The store.** A lean-to of dressed stone with a timber frame against a cut face four days up the middle passage, no roof on the north side worth the name, **eleven years of beech, forty-one pieces of which eleven are better than the rest, two hundred and six pieces of fir in three grades, one beam entered as a dash.** The list is **fourteen lines with no date on it**, and it has been read out twice and compared against what came down the passage, and **the two agree, and that is the first time in thirty-one weeks that a document in this building and a fact in a room have been the same thing.** **The beech is in this anteroom: six cut pieces on a bench. It came down four days of road on four men's backs with no seal, no form and no name.** **The terms stand: no name of his on any paper of this boundary's, the whole list read in a room on request, and he can stop. He is going up the passage on the Monday of the second week of the thirty-first week with three of his own and the fir, and the beech stays.**
- **The brace.** **Not started, and not to be started until the condition is met.** It is to go across the head of the anteroom's outer service door frame, which is a finger and three-quarters out of true, packed in stone in clay, holding and not fixed, with the Marker mark off the wall and a carpenter in the room who would not put it back. **Its counter-bearing is the party wall the schoolroom leans on, and it is forbidden at the low end of the corridor because a brace that reacts at the lowest point in a building is a brace that puts a person there and the third condition on the blank page says that nobody is to be sent anywhere to make it true.** Its bearing mark is **not his and is not chalk: it is four marks and one groove in the lintel above the open school corner's mouth, read three ways this month, one of the four being two strokes, the four damaged and the groove undamaged, and it has not said anybody its limit.** Its joinwood is **two wedges out of a school-door head and two beech lengths out of a school store.** Its condition is written and **is not met.** Its first cost has already been paid: **the school door has no wedge in it and the answer to a door that has to be watched is a person and there is not one available.**
- **Wedges: seven sound and one dead stick, and the two at the school-door head are now in the brace.** Two in the school corner's door, three at the waist with the third up a quarter of an inch and a bit and not touched, one in the basket, one dead stick that has been missing since Batch 0003. **The school door has none and nobody has put a hand on the waist's third one.**
- **The waist.** **The water went off the fill on the second day of the thirtieth week and has not been on it since, and no chapter in this batch says it came back or came up again, and nobody in this building has said why.** The third wedge is up a quarter of an inch and a bit and was not touched. **And Jo Verrick has given a figure at the waist on purpose for the first time in thirty-one weeks: two inches and a bit of movement in a party wall over about an hour, and it is in a clerk's book and not on a page, because the plan has no row for that wall and the refusal of a row stands and was argued in three hands in the twenty-seventh week.**
- **The pot: nine days.** It was ten and had not gone up since the fifth day of the nineteenth week, and it **went down on the fourth day of the thirty-first week**, because the seam gave one jar where it gives two, and **because a dead man's share has been going back into it since the fourth day of the twenty-fifth week on purpose and the cook has said out loud in a room that she is not going to do it for a brace.** Jo Verrick says the seam is at four threads and was eight in the nineteenth week and has not been measured in between, and **there is no number for how fast that is going in this building and the clerk has entered that she is not going to be the one who makes one.** Nothing in this batch fixes it.
- **The jars: two a day, entered separately and never added to the pot, with one on the second day of the thirtieth week.** The seam gave four threads at a first bell.
- **Crew hours: sixty-six and a half, up three from sixty-three and a half**, on the third day of the thirtieth week, on a named asking in a room with the terms out loud and Renn Vale named as the person who can be told no and the three who could not be asked named with a third set of reasons. **Two hours of Iven Tallo's and one hour of Marek Venn's, and the split was named before anybody was asked, by the carpenter.** **The crew have now been asked four times in thirty-one weeks and the reasons have not been the same reasons twice.**
- **Boundary hours: a hundred and seventeen and a quarter, plus a hundred and ninety-three, = three hundred and ten and a quarter.** The list adds **by the day the work was done**: **three, two, four, three, two in the thirtieth week; three, four, two, three, two in the thirty-first**, summing to twenty-eight. **Running figures with the day's increment and the total are read out in five chapters — 151, 153, 155, 158 and 160 — and are entered and not read out in 152, 154, 156, 157 and 159, each with a reason in a clerk's hand and each reason specific to that morning and none of them improved by saying it was busy.** The base is the constant one hundred and seventeen and a quarter, the total is monotonic, both columns reconcile, **and the reconciliation counter is re-derived once, at the start of this batch, from twelve weeks in the twenty-ninth week: it is thirteen in the thirtieth week and fourteen in the thirty-first, and it is not re-derived again inside the batch.**
- **The plan on butcher's paper: sixteen rows, in four parts, in a coat, with a date in the top corner, no name anywhere and no signature anywhere at all, and it does not leave this building.** The first column holds a permitted occupancy and has never held a count of anybody. The second column has sixteen conditions with no number and no day on any of them. The third column is fifteen lines filled and one empty that the keeper will not name. The fourth column is empty and unheaded with its reason in two hands. **The tenth row's second column now carries a condition that is about a brace and not about a room, with two people in it, and the clerk read it against what was said and compared the two.** **The plan has no row for the party wall the brace pushes on, because that wall is not a room anybody is going to be in, and the refusal of a row for the low end of the corridor stands and was not re-argued in this batch.** No chapter put the school back in the school corner.
- **The board: seventeen entries — eleven nulls, one claim, one mark, one row, a plan, a condition and a store; nine in the ordinary column and eight in the column with nothing over the top of it.** The claim's condition has not been met. The mark's entry is still on the board. **The seventeenth is a store, entered by the count himself, in the unheaded column, and it is the first thing on that board that is not in this building.** The column is unheaded for the ninth consecutive batch and the ninth reason was given once, on the fourth day of the thirtieth week, and is not to be improved on. **The board is walked in four chapters — 151, 154, 158 and 160 — and not in the other six, with a reason in a clerk's hand or in the count's own words in each of the six, and in no chapter is it walked by the clerk and the count both on the same day for the same reason.**
- **The instruments.** A market form back on a board with a new seal number. A carrier's paper with seven lines and two of them for a person, refused. A route-holder's post with a name in a register, in a stiff cover with a proper seal, **not refused and not accepted.** A printed Compact schedule with a column headed for the person who will be present, **struck in nine seconds with the three rooms under it, and a third room left standing that a carrier explained in a room.** A store's list of fourteen lines with no date on it and a dash on the ninth. **The charter, the plan and the first column are unchanged and none of them left this building in this batch, and no paper went down the middle passage in this batch except a carrier's own not-refusal on the outside of a stiff cover.**
- **Unchanged and carried:** the anteroom frame; the second way out of the anteroom as a word; the ninth room, the four treads and the ninth line's *none*; the four households at a second gate; the review page at the low end, blank, with three conditions, the third of them saying that nobody is to be sent anywhere to make it true; the wash-room condition; the wrapped plate, unopened for thirty-one weeks; the brass rule, silent for longer than twenty-two weeks; Form 9-C; Form 7-B; the bundle in the anteroom; and a person under a lip, packed and not held, with a post that has never been built.

## Measured craft baseline, for Volume 04 Batch 0002

**Counted on the delivered files of `chapters/volume-04/chapter-0151.md` through `chapter-0160.md` by the method set out below. These are counted, not estimated. The method is stated with its exclusions in the same sentence as its figures, because Batch 0004's review set that rule and Batch 0004's own record failed it, and because Batch 0005's recorded figures could not be reproduced on the delivered files — which is itself a finding and is recorded as one below.**

**These figures were counted twice.** They were counted on the first delivery and again after a review-fix pass that repaired the batch's prose register, and **every paragraph and divider figure below changed, and the reason they changed is that the first count was taken on a delivery that has since been edited.** The narrative-share and sentence-length figures are new and were not measured at all before the fix pass, which is the finding `reviews/volume-04/batch-0001.md` Finding 1 records: **a chapter can pass every check this file lists and still read as a transcript of a meeting.** Batch 0002 must count all of them.

**Narrative share and sentence length, counted on the delivered files, with the method stated because the first attempt at it was wrong.** Quoted words are every word inside a matched pair of quotation marks, as a percentage of all words in the file, per chapter: **66, 61, 72, 74, 66, 76, 71, 70, 74, 64, an average of 69.4.** A sentence boundary is taken **inside** each paragraph, after a full stop, a question mark or an exclamation mark followed by whitespace, and quotation marks are stripped before the count; **the first attempt ran the split over each whole file, which merged paragraphs across a blank line and produced a false longest sentence of 415 words in Chapter 160 and a false batch mean of 47 to 76 by chapter.** Measured correctly: **mean sentence 31.1 words, median 30, longest 97, three sentences of ninety words or more out of 1,180, and 131 of 1,180 over sixty words. Median paragraph 50 words, longest 131. The batch as first delivered was a mean of 33.2 with twenty-six sentences of ninety words or more and a longest of 134.** **Batch 0002 inherits a ceiling of seventy per cent quoted words by chapter, a mean sentence under thirty, and no sentence over ninety words, and is told to reach them with staging and shorter speeches and not with fewer speeches, because the chapters whose subject is a set of terms being stated are supposed to be mostly quotation.**

**The method.** A paragraph is a block of the raw file split on blank lines. The ten `# Chapter NNN` title lines are not paragraphs and are excluded. A paragraph that begins with a quotation mark is dialogue and is excluded from the paragraph and opener counts. **The `---` section rules are counted as non-bold non-dialogue paragraphs, as Batch 0004 set, and no paragraph consisting only of rule characters is dropped from the duplication measure.** Bold markers are left in place, so a bolded narrative opener counts as a bold opener. For the duplication measure, **bold section-divider paragraphs are excluded, per Batch 0005's exclusion 3**, and the measure is on non-bold content paragraphs only.

- **The method did not reproduce Batch 0005's recorded figures and this is recorded rather than hidden.** Run against the delivered files of Chapters 141–150 it returns **200 non-dialogue paragraphs, 105 not bold-opened, 95 bold section-divider openers, openers including bold *The* 3, *Nobody* 3, *And* 4, *She* 4, *He* 4 (eighteen together) and excluding bold *The* 3, *Nobody* 2, *And* 0, *She* 4, *He* 3 (twelve together)**, against the recorded 235 / 142 / 93, 4 / 4 / 0 / 3 / 4 and 4 / 3 / 0 / 3 / 3. **The figures that do reproduce are the ones that matter and the figures that do not are the paragraph count and the bold-divider count.** **A later phase must validate the method against **the delivered files of this batch, `chapters/volume-04/chapter-0151.md` through `chapter-0160.md`**, and not against Batch 0004's or Batch 0005's, and must state its exclusions with its figure, and must not carry any superseded paragraph figure from this file or from Batch 0005 forward as if it were measured. Every paragraph and divider figure in this section was superseded once already by a review-fix pass, which is the proof that a carried-forward figure decays.**
- **Body paragraphs, excluding the chapter heading and the horizontal rules: 741. Paragraphs that do not begin with a quotation mark: 214. Paragraphs that do not begin with a bold marker: 674. Fully-bold paragraphs of any length: 66, of which 38 are under three hundred characters. These four figures supersede the 179 / 116 / 63 this line carried before the review-fix pass, which were taken on the first pass and not on the delivery.**
- **Openers, excluding bolded paragraphs:** *The* 7, *Nobody* 7, *And* 4, *She* 12, *He* 11, **forty-one together. Including bolded paragraphs:** *The* 9, *Nobody* 8, *And* 5, *She* 12, *He* 11, **forty-five together.** Batch 0005's including figure was 4 / 4 / 0 / 3 / 4 and Batch 0004's was 4 / 1 / 11 / 7 / 3. **The *And* figure is the batch's real result: five, against a target of under eleven, and it was reached by rewriting fifty-one openers in the writing session and twelve more in the review-fix pass into other shapes rather than by deleting anything. The second pass was needed because fifteen narration paragraphs were added and twelve of them began *The* or *Nobody*, which pushed those two figures to eighteen and eleven before they were rewritten again.**
- **The divider count was reported as 63 against Batch 0005's recorded 93 and Batch 0004's recorded 78, and that figure cannot be reproduced under any definition this file states. Measured on the delivered files it is 66 fully-bold paragraphs, of which 38 are under three hundred characters, which is the natural reading of a section divider; the old number was almost certainly a first-pass figure carried into the record as though it were a measurement of the delivery. It is corrected to 66 and 38 with the definition stated, and the rule for Batch 0002 is that a divider figure is worthless without its definition and its exclusions in the same sentence.** The original claim that it fell without a beat being cut, because forty-one bold dividers were rewritten, is not verifiable from the delivered files and is withdrawn.
- **Markup: every one of the ten files is even on asterisks and on quotation marks, every paragraph has an even quotation count and an even bold count, there is no run of three or more asterisks, and there is no doubled quotation mark.** **This was measured, and it was wrong twice in the writing session and both defects were repaired: a speech-paragraph bold span was left crossing a sentence of narration, and a merge of two speeches dropped a bold marker and produced four doubled quotation marks in Chapter 160.** The lesson for Batch 0002 is Batch 0003's and Batch 0004's lesson for the third time: **the form is countable and must be checked before delivery, and a repair made by paragraph index will break it, so the files must be re-measured from disk after any structural edit.**
- **Duplicated non-bold content paragraphs on a ninety-character prefix: zero, and the exclusion is stated in the same sentence as the figure — the excluded class is fully-bold divider paragraphs, which is the sixth-bell house formula.** Measured without that exclusion there are two groups, both of them the sixth-bell formula, in 154/156/157/159 and in 155/158/160. **What this line previously described as a school-morning paragraph shared between two chapters is not one: it is the third of the three reasons for not asking somebody, in Dalla Sen's mouth in 153 and Nera Oduya's in 156, and the sentence the two shared was the description of Tams Quill's right hand. It was rewritten in 156 in the review-fix pass and the group is gone.** A count read-out paragraph shared by 158 and 160 was rewritten in the writing session.
- **Viewpoint presence: the protagonist is named in ten of ten chapters, with interior life in ten of ten, and this was measured rather than asserted.** He was named in nine on first measure, absent from Chapter 155, and a beat was written into that chapter and two more into 154 and 159, the latter two for Renn Vale, who was also absent from them.

The figures this line carried before that pass were wrong and are corrected here: it said 2,837 to 4,169 and 35,694 with Chapter 159 at 4,035 and Chapter 160 at 4,169, and the range and total were right while the per-chapter attribution was not — the delivered files before the pass were Chapter 159 at 4,169 and Chapter 160 at 4,131. Two chapters are over the soft ceiling of 4,000, 159 at 4,294 and 160 at 4,187, which are the same two as before the pass, and they are recorded here rather than quietly delivered.** The reason is the one this batch has given all along: Chapter 160 carries the volume's question, four attempts at an answer, a clerk's third refusal, a sixth-bell read and a closing image; Chapter 159 carries three complete scenes and a condition; Chapter 156 carries two askings in full with the terms of each. **The first pass used the *And* and *The* figures as its levers instead of deletion. The review-fix pass used the sentence structure as its lever: it broke every sentence of ninety words or more at its own clause boundaries, which removed about 1,400 words of run-together prose, and it put back 1,279 words of physical staging, and the net is the batch above.**
- **Meta language: zero instances of *chapter*, *volume*, *this chapter*, *this volume*, *this batch* or *the reader* in narration.** One meta leak was written and cut: *Then the room tried, and the trying is the rest of the chapter.* **The licensed uses of *in this book* are all in a mouth or in the clerk's own entry — Renn Vale's in Chapter 160, Mero Senn's in Chapter 159, the carrier's and the clerk's in Chapters 152, 158 and 160 — and one narration use was cut from Chapter 158, where four men carried the beech down a passage *who are not named in this book*.**
- **People: Talla Roke and Pell Roke are not in the same room in any chapter of the batch.** She is in 151, 152, 155, 156, 157 and 160; he is in 153, 156 and 158. **The batch card placed him in 157 and 160 and the card has been corrected to the delivery, with the reason recorded in the card file.**

## Open hand-off

**Volume 04's central pressure is identified and not yet answered, and the batch's immediate business is not finished.** A market form is back on a board with a new seal number and nobody put it up. A guild load's paper has been read out and refused, and a cart is at a gate. **A brace is designed, costed, argued in public, and not built, because a store's keeper has to be asked before a beech is a brace's joinwood and because the condition on the brace is not met.** A physician has asked a clerk to read a wall every morning for a fortnight. A schoolteacher has written a condition with two people in it. A cook has said a number out loud and it went down. A route-holder's post is in a carrier's hand, going down four days of a road to a camp that took a halfpenny sheet.

**And the positions the next batch stands on, on the page, with nobody in the room having seen them:** the wood is in the anteroom and the brace is not started; the school door has no wedge and the answer to a door that has to be watched is a person and there is not one; **the reaction goes on the wall the schoolroom leans on, and the survey camp's finding that the wall is sound is what makes the brace possible and the same sheet is the reason nobody has come back in a fortnight**; **the mark is damaged and reads three ways and has not said anybody its limit, so the limit has to come from the person the brace can hurt**; **a clerk has refused three times in a month to put two questions next to each other, and a man has refused twice in a room to say which two walls he would let a brace push on, and both of those are the same argument from opposite ends**; and a brass survey rule on a hook in a coat has said nothing for longer than twenty-two weeks and a wrapped plate on a shelf above the same bench has been unopened for thirty-one.

**And the question, asked out loud in a room in four words and not answered: who owns a wall. It is on the page, it is in a clerk's book in the asker's own words, and nobody in that room is going to answer it in this batch's successor either without costing something.**

---

# Batch Summary — Volume 03, Batch 0005 (Chapters 141–150)

## Outcome

**Volume 03, Batch 0005 is complete, and with it Volume 03. Chapters 141–150 exist in `chapters/volume-03/` as finished prose in close third person on Marek Venn, with no viewpoint shift anywhere in the batch.** The batch carries the volume's climax, its resolution and its question: **a plan of rooms became a network of four rooms and a door, designed in the open out of a page nobody signed, and the first thing that turned out to be missing was not a design but a pair of hands and a crew that is not on a rota; the crew were asked a third time in a room with the terms out loud and the three who could not be asked were named with reasons that had changed; a condition on a room that was only checked three mornings in seven was found to have a hole in it by the woman who wrote it, and the answer was four sentences a week in an empty room; a courier's rule was tested on a road and a bundle could not leave this building because nobody would fill in two lines; a Compact procedure that assumed this boundary wanted to be looked at was decided upon, and a title in a field book was left wrong on purpose; a clerk noticed that the air engineer had stopped making a man say how he knows and the air engineer answered about the work; a network of four rooms was found to be unusable on any day of the week because the one place that could have made it the whole building has no row; a man went down into a channel at the lowest point in this building with a lamp in his left hand to clear the silt out of it by hand, under a limit with no number and no day on it, stated by the only person who may stop it, and the limit was not met and the holding was stopped anyway by a woman turning a wheel she had not turned in twenty-nine weeks; he was brought out of the channel and then out of the building the ordinary way, in daylight, in front of everybody, and the second way out of the room he came out of was a word he said himself and it opened nothing; the room that was shut is open under a public occupancy plan that is the same page it has always been, read out loud to a person from another office with no copy given and a person named in a room who may stop it; a form came off a board at a gate because a woman at a counter read a stranger's mark and decided that a thing a mark can be used on is not exclusive, and the office conceded nothing in a word; a surface federation brought six pages with a clause in them that says a room whose condition is not being checked may be entered and the person who keeps it cannot refuse, and nobody was defeated; and a courier asked the volume's question out loud in a room, in the shape the withdrawal created, and nobody answered it.**

The batch's spine, as delivered: **a plan of rooms is not a network of rooms, and the difference is four women and a man who keeps a page in a coat and a clerk with a piece of chalk; the last one out of a room decides when the room is empty and that is the only order in it; a word is not a way out and a person is, and a woman who has spent four weeks being a handle does not get to choose the shape of the handle; a man who holds a hole is a person who has to be brought out of a room in daylight in front of the people he was holding it for; nobody sent him and the difference between that and the twenty-fifth week was the smallest thing in the book and the clerk said so; and the batch's two documents defeated themselves — the plan became public because a surface federation's own procedure said it could be shown and not taken, and a guild's form came off a board because the guild's own rule could not use a stranger's mark.**

**Four things the batch refused, deliberately. No System panel of any kind** — no stat block, no item table, no quest text, no experience bar, no rank, and the correct amount of System in this batch is none. **No power stage** — the Marker is spent, the right hand's fine control is not recovered, the Reader reading is not repeatable, the brass rule is silent, and **the whole batch is ordinary civil engineering done in public: a lamp, a left hand, a channel with silt in it, two flags and a length of spent line, a wheel on a gate, and a door packed with stone and clay.** **No mark cut in the stone anywhere in this building** — the four marks and the groove on the lintel above the open school corner's mouth are read two ways by two people, answer nothing, and help nobody. **The eleven and the ten were not joined**, and neither was the blank page and the row that was never written: the two sat next to each other in a clerk's book on purpose, with a reason, and the clerk entered that she chose not to join them and then entered on Tuesday that the difference between the two halves of her own entry was smaller than it looked like.

## Movement

- **Chapter 141 — A Bundle And A Name In A Space. Twenty-eighth week, first day, a Monday, the corridor shut, the school did not run.** **The market day was the sixth day of the week before and it came and went, and a courier came back up the passage with what it cost: a man from this Hearthhold was turned back at a gate because he would not give a name, and a bundle is still in the anteroom, and the cook has been putting his share of the pot back into the pot since the fourth day of the twenty-fifth week and did it on purpose and told nobody.** Renn Vale found a hole in her own condition — a room used three days in seven has its condition checked three mornings in seven — and said so before anybody found it, and gave the answer: four sentences a week in an empty room, which costs nothing, and which she says is stupid and cheap. Ilsa Norr agreed and put the cost in the book as a preference and not as a noble position. **Marek Venn said the word *network* out loud first, in a room, and said the first thing missing from one is not a design and it is a pair of hands and a crew that is not on a rota.** Tams Quill gave the **eighth** reason for refusing to head the column, and it is worse: a heading is a place to keep the person who works under it. Board walked.
- **Chapter 142 — Each Room Into The Next. Second day, a Tuesday, the corridor shut.** **The network was designed in the open, out of the third column, one row at a time, and it is multi-room by construction: the kitchen, the room at the kitchen-line end, the common landing and the anteroom, each of which can be emptied into the next, each with its own count and its own person who keeps it, and the person who keeps each one wrote their own condition.** Mikka Hale wrote permissions and said she had counted nobody. Renn Vale wrote a condition with no number and no day on it and put the word *waits* into it for the fifth person at the waist. Ilsa Norr stood up and said the network is four rooms and a child on a step. Bel Ordry refused a number on the landing because the stair is not in the charter, the plan or the second sheet. **Nera Oduya struck the wash rooms out of the list in nine seconds and gave no reason and nobody asked.** Talla Roke stated her test and it is a test, not a rescue: no order, nobody at the front, and she will walk it as an ordinary resident with a bundle that is not hers. **Marek Venn said the network ends at a door, and that the only place that could have made it the whole of this building has no row, and the refusal stands and is not re-argued and both halves of it are in a clerk's book in three hands.** Figures given; the board was not walked and the clerk entered why.
- **Chapter 143 — The Third Asking. Third day, a Wednesday, the corridor open, the school ran for the nineteenth morning.** **A clerk asked the crew for a third time in twenty-eight weeks, in a room, with the terms out loud, Renn Vale named as the person who can be told no, and the three who could not be asked named with the reason for each before the asking, and the reasons are not the reasons she gave last month: Leena Orr, because she is the person who will have to answer whether a person can stand at the low end; Pell Roke, because he is fourteen and because a count taken by the man who reads numbers is a count nobody can argue with; Tams Quill, because if he also walks the network then the same man is the hands, the witness and the record.** Four hours, two of Iven Tallo's and two of Marek Venn's, four rooms, nothing opened, nothing moved, no hand near the waist's third wedge. **Iven Tallo struck the word *brace* out of the back of a day-board in a carpenter's own words, because a brace is a support joined to a mark and there is no mark in it and a thing called temporary is a thing a man in a bad month is allowed to stop maintaining.** Two pairs of hands walked the four rooms and found that **three of the four second ways out are rooms and the fourth is a word, and a word is not a way out, and this network has a second way out and it is the same word it was on the Friday in the twenty-fourth week.** The crew's column moved by four, from fifty-nine and a half to sixty-three and a half. Board walked.
- **Chapter 144 — The Person It Is About Is In The Room. Fourth day, a Thursday, the corridor shut, the school did not run.** **Hollis Ashe's four pages were read out in an anteroom and decided upon, and the decision was that three of them are given and one is refused and one of the six in a later document is correct, and the boundary discovered that the document written for a stranger is the only instrument that can make its own page public without a name on it.** **A reply sheet went back into a coat unaltered: the line wants a name or a description sufficient to identify, the description exists in a field book four days up the middle passage, it is a title, and the person it describes was standing on the landing with her arms folded, and Renn Vale picked the half where the title stays wrong on purpose and said the third and best thing is available and she is not going to ask for it, because a woman who has spent four weeks being a handle does not get to choose the shape of the handle.** **A clerk noticed out loud, in a room with a stranger in it, that the air engineer has stopped saying *say how you know* to a man, and said she was noticing it and was not going to say the words, and Nera Oduya answered about the work: there are two handholds on that page now, one a man's and one a room's, and a schoolteacher's hand in a column cannot be adjusted by anybody who finds it inconvenient, and if she made him defend himself in front of a room that has a room in it she would be telling the room the man is what can be doubted and the room is not.** The network was then found unusable on any day of the week, and the collision was staged as a collision in a room and not as a technicality: the blank page, three conditions, the third of them saying that nobody is to be sent anywhere to make it true, and nobody going to ask the woman who wrote it. **Marek Venn offered to go, and said out loud in front of a clerk and a stranger that he is the only person in this building who does not keep a room and is not on a rota and is not a resident of it by anything except standing in it, and that he is not a post and no page is going to get his name on it; and Dalla Sen entered that nobody sent him as two entries and not as one, and entered that the difference between that and the twenty-fifth week was the smallest thing in her book and that she was entering the difference as a difference.** Figures not read out and the reason entered; the board was not walked and the reason was that a sheet from a surface office with a title on it was lying on the table.
- **Chapter 145 — A Person Holding An Opening. Fifth day, a Friday, the corridor open, the school ran for the twentieth morning.** **The schoolteacher agreed to say the strip-under-the-stone sentence out loud in an empty room four mornings a week, and put the cost in the book in her own words: a woman saying a sentence to a building about a stone nobody is standing near, with nothing to notice her stopping.** **Nera Oduya stated the limit on the holding with no number and no day on it — the opening is held while there is water standing on the fill at the waist — and named herself as the person who may stop it, and said out loud that she has never used the air gate and that she is telling the room in advance so that it is on the page before anybody needs it, and that the air at that end goes down the channel before the water does.** **At the fourth bell Marek Venn went down into the channel with a lamp in his left hand and said out loud, to nobody, in a corridor, that there is no mark in it, that he is not being sent, and that the limit has a second half and the second half is him; and the clerk entered a man's days in the boundary's column and not the crew's, and gave the reason out loud, and entered that the page that says somebody stands at the low end is still blank and that it will be blank when he comes up the steps.** Figures given; the board was not walked and the reason was a limit and a hole.
- **Chapter 146 — The First Time She Shut The Air Gate. Twenty-ninth week, first day, a Monday, the corridor shut by the arrangement, the school did not run and did not lose anything.** **Renn Vale wrote a change to a room people use in her own hand, with a condition and no number and no day on it, and the clerk put it against the original and compared the two, and the corridor was open on a day the arrangement shuts it while there is a person at the low end of it, and no closure was removed and no school day was restored.** The network was used for the first time and there was no order on it: the last one out of each room decided when that room was empty, and the person who decided was a woman of sixty-eight who is not named in this book. **Nera Oduya opened the uncertified mouth in the school corner as a vent for two hours on her own authority, said out loud in a room that nobody was to write down after today that the mouth is fine, and at the third bell the water came up the channel, the air in it went out from under a man, and she turned a wheel she had not turned in twenty-nine weeks and shut the air gate — and said out loud, before anybody could say it after her, that she was not doing it for him.** The water went down the channel. Figures not read out and the reason entered; the board was not walked.
- **Chapter 147 — The Word Is Not A Way Out. Second day, a Tuesday, the corridor shut, the school did not run.** Renn Vale opened the corridor again on the same term. **The air engineer stopped the holding and the limit had not been met, and she said both halves in that order: the water is on the fill, the holding is not out of condition, nobody is entitled to say it has failed, and she is stopping it anyway because a person in a channel cannot be told anything and cannot be known to be in one, and a holding whose only condition is on the other side of the floor from the man is not a holding.** **He got himself up out of the channel and then walked the length of the corridor on his own, past the dip and the school door and down nine steps, and the anteroom's terms were read out, and he said the word and the clerk repeated it, and it opened nothing, and he went out of the door on his own two feet in front of everybody.** **Renn Vale said the sentence the volume has been walking toward for four weeks: a word is not a way out, a person is, she has been signing a rule that says a person does not count, and she is not taking the word out of the terms of a room because nine people in this building have been told that saying it made them safe.** **Mero Senn said it was not a good thing that happened, it was a thing that went well, and that in a year somebody will write a sentence about it that makes it sound like a decision, and that only one of the two things is going to be in the sentence.** Marek Venn said he cannot go down there again, that he does not know why, that he is not going to find out, and that a man who says *I could probably do it again* in a room in a month is the man who recommended that a room be shut in the twenty-fourth week. Nothing was forgiven and nothing was repaired. Figures given; board walked at fifteen entries.
- **Chapter 148 — A Room Kept Open, And A Form Off A Board. Third day, a Wednesday, the corridor open, the school ran for the twenty-first morning.** **The boundary made its own page public with a surface federation's own instrument: the plan was read out in four parts in an anteroom to a person from another office, with no copy given, and it is the same page it has always been, extended by four rows into a network, and a date in the top corner and no name anywhere and no signature anywhere at all.** The first column took the same number four times and not four numbers, and the cook said why, and said for the fifth time that she has counted nobody. **Renn Vale said her own name out loud in a room as the person who may stop it, and it went in a clerk's book in a coat and not on the page, and Nera Oduya said the half about a name and a title that she had said once and was not going to say twice.** **Mero Senn put his name against a refusal to certify the room at the kitchen-line end, in a clerk's book, which is the first thing he has put anywhere in twenty-nine weeks, and gave the reason: arguable is worth more than safe in a building like this and he is not going to be the man who says it is worth more than safe anywhere else.** **And a form came off a board at a gate four days up the middle passage, because a woman at a counter read a courier's mark, found that it is three people's and cannot be entered in a register, and decided that a thing a stranger's mark can be used on is not exclusive — and said four other things, which were that the board is a courtesy, that the road is unchanged, that the seal stands, and that a sheet comes back on a board in a month by a clerk who does not know about Tuesday.** A courier said out loud in an anteroom that it was not a win. **A sixteenth entry went on the board: a condition, in the column with nothing over the top of it, because it was tested and it held.** Figures given; the board was not walked, and the count gave the reason.
- **Chapter 149 — A Regime Is Not A Threat. Fourth day, a Thursday, the corridor shut, the school did not run.** **Pettin Rahl came up the middle passage with six pages he did not write and does not agree with all of, and said so before he sat down, and read the fifth clause out loud: a room whose condition is not being checked at the time the room is in use may be entered, and the boundary may not refuse, and the refusal of the person who keeps the room is not a ground.** Renn Vale said that is not an inspection and that the word should be used correctly, and that the only clause in the document that is not about a room is about a person being unable to say no. **The hardliner's case was given at length and was good, and it was given in a room where nobody argued with it: a number in a column that nobody has counted is a number that has to be believed, and his belief in this boundary is worth nothing, because he is going to carry six pages up a passage and put them in front of a competent and tired man in a cell on a Tuesday.** A schoolteacher gave him the shape of a count and not the number, and said that the reason she will not give it away is not caution. **Nera Oduya struck a room out of a printed schedule in nine seconds and gave no reason at all and nobody asked.** Bel Ordry answered three clauses out loud, refused the fourth, and said the sixth is correct and that it is the reason he has been right about one thing in twenty-nine weeks. **A federation's route inspector said out loud, against her own office, that the instrument which made this boundary arguable is hers and that she knew the price of it when she wrote it, and that she is still glad she wrote it.** Nobody was defeated, nobody conceded in a word, and a regime is a schedule and not a refusal. Figures not read out and the reason entered; the board was not walked.
- **Chapter 150 — The Question With Thirty People On It. Fifth day, a Friday, the corridor open, the school ran for the twenty-second morning.** **A boy of fourteen read all sixteen numbers in the first column out loud in one breath and said nothing about any of them, and said there was nothing in the fourth column.** **Talla Roke asked the volume's question out loud, in the shape the withdrawal created: she has got a road, thirty-one people went up it as ordinary residents with nobody at the front of it, and the form came off the board at the end of it, and she is not going to pretend the second thing happened because of the first; and she asked it in the same words as the first time, because she has changed twice this month and is not going to change the wording. Nobody answered it.** Nera Oduya said she has half of it and gave the same three sentences and did not give it, and her condition is not met, and nobody asked her. **Dalla Sen entered that she was asked once to put two questions next to each other and refused, and that nobody asked her to this time, and that a week is not a month, and wrote that sentence twice so that the second one is the one a person reads.** The board was walked at sixteen entries and the column is still unheaded for the eighth batch, and the count gave no new reason. **The clerk asked a man in a room what he is going to do when the weather turns, and he did not answer, and she did not ask him twice, and she entered it as a question and not as a finding.** The count was read at eighty-five.
