The chapter closes on the volume's immediate promise answered and its larger question open.

## Required character, relationship and power change

- **Power: nothing advanced, and the correct amount of System in this batch is no panel at all.** No bearing mark, no route change as a stage, no binding, no relay, no brace, and **no supernatural act of any kind.** The occupancy plan is a page. **A room became usable in this batch because a woman said a sentence to an empty room, a cook wrote a number she did not count, a clerk compared a term against an original, a carpenter struck a word out of a plan, an air engineer turned a wheel, and a man cleared silt out of a channel with his hands.** The plan did not decide where anybody went, did not open a door and did not make a room safe; the air gate opened a thing that was already open, and the door he came out of was opened by his own hand. The right hand's fine control is not recovered, the left hand will not close after the holding and did not close before it, the brass rule is silent, the Reader claim's condition is still unmet, the four marks and the groove answer nothing, and no mark was cut in the stone.
- **Nera Oduya: the third of Volume 03's five acts of trust is spent, and it is the one Batch 0003 set up and Batch 0004 held.** **She stops checking his arithmetic out loud and somebody notices and she gives an answer about the work and not about him.** A clerk says out loud in a room with a stranger in it that she has been going to say *say how you know* to that man for four weeks and has not said it once this month, that he has given her two figures and she has read both against the original and asked him nothing, and that she noticed and he did not; and she refuses to say the words, because a stranger is in the room. **Nera Oduya's answer is entirely about the work: on the third day of the twenty-fourth week his figure was the only figure in the room with nothing to agree with, and there are two handholds on that page now, one a man's and one a room's, and a schoolteacher's hand in a column cannot be adjusted by anybody who finds it inconvenient, so making him defend himself in front of a room that has a room in it would be telling the room that the man is what can be doubted and the room is not.** Nobody thanked her, a woman who might have said a kind thing did not, and it is not a recognition scene and there is no scene between them. **In the climax she moves people, she holds the air, she opens an uncertified mouth as a vent for two hours on her own authority, she turns the air gate for the first time in twenty-nine weeks, and she stops a holding whose limit was not met — and she says out loud, before anybody can say it after her, that she is not doing it for him.** She is wrong in public about a page once, in Chapter 148, when she wants a row for her four pages and then declines to ask for it. **She does not say her theory, her condition is approached and is not met, and no room asks her.** She does not leave and no chapter stages her going and the question of her going is not raised.
- **The working practice holds in every record, and the batch is its proof and its limit again.** The person who keeps a room writes the change to it: **four room-keepers wrote four conditions in four hands for a network; Renn Vale wrote a change to a corridor she keeps, on a shut day, and it was put against the original and compared; the air engineer wrote the limit and a second person read it against what she had said and she compared the two herself; the schoolteacher wrote her own third column and said yes to it standing; the cook wrote four numbers she did not count; a courier's rule went into a clerk's book in her own words; a woman with a rule struck a room out of a list in nine seconds and gave no reason; a physician put his name against a refusal in a clerk's book and not on a page.** And the limit is once more the limit: **a person who may stop the network is named in a room and not on a page, and a man who held the opening is entered in advance as not a post, in a clerk's book, twice.**
- **Talla Roke is in the building, on her own terms, with her own three named people, and is not a rescue.** She brought the fact of the market day, and the bundle, and the fact that a man from this Hearthhold could not use a road he lives behind. **She set the test the evacuation had to pass — as ordinary residents, with no order on it and nobody at the front — and it passed, and thirty-one people went up a market road on the second day of the twenty-ninth week, and her three and she were four people out of thirty-five.** She is not sending anybody and said so in a room. She is not offering her brother anything and he is not used as a route to her; **he is named in two chapters of this batch, as one of the three who could not be asked and reading the first column aloud, and she is in neither of those two, and no chapter in this batch puts the two of them in the same room.** **Her mark on a paper is a courier's mark and not a guild's, and it came off a board at a counter because a mark cannot be entered in a register, and she said out loud in an anteroom that it was not a win and that a competent person told them how long their own rule lasts.** Her spring is owed by nobody and was not paid off as a gift and is named once in the hand-over.
- **Mero Senn is the instrument of the rule and not a rescue.** He was asked a **different question** — not whether a plan with a number in it is acceptable, which he has now been asked five times and to which he has given the same answer five times, but whether there is a version of this where nobody is in that channel — and he said no, and said he was not going to make the search sound like a principle. **He refused to let a thing that went well be read as a good thing, in a room, on the day it went well.** He put his name against a refusal in a clerk's book for the first time in the book. He does not fix anything and softens nobody and he was wrong in public and not consoled.
- **Bel Ordry:** kept the page in a coat and said the reason out loud before he opened it, and **turned his own sentence of the twenty-sixth week the other way round — he would rather a federation want to look at this boundary than be able to take it, and a page can be read to a room and not taken out of it, and that is in the third sentence of a procedure he did not write.** He refused a number on the landing. He asked the physician a question nobody had asked him. He asked for a correction and was refused one and entered the refusal. He still says a plan in a coat is not a system and that he is the only one who has to say it.
- **Dalla Sen:** named the three who could not be asked before asking anybody; **noticed out loud that the air engineer had stopped making a man say how he knows and said she was noticing it and was not going to say the words;** entered that the title stays wrong as a decision and not as an oversight; entered that nobody sent him as two entries and then, on Tuesday, entered that the difference between the two halves was the smallest thing in her book; entered a man's hours in the boundary's column and gave the reason out loud; entered that a page is still blank; entered the sixteenth thing on the board herself; entered the four Compact clauses answered, refused and agreed; **asked a man in a room what he is going to do when the weather turns and entered that he did not answer and that she did not ask him twice;** and kept writing a dead man's count every day and said out loud in four weeks running that she is going to keep writing it and that nobody has thanked her and that a woman from another office read it and did not say a kind thing about it and that she was glad.
- **Tams Quill:** **the crew's column moved by four on a named asking in a room with the terms out loud**, and he gave the **eighth** reason for refusing to head the column, and it is worse, and it is not about a word: **a heading is a place to keep the person who works under it**, because a null is a reading that found nothing and eleven of them are things somebody was paid to go and look for and did not find, and under a heading they become eleven findings, and a finding is a thing a person is responsible for. He was named as one of the three who could not be asked, with the reason that if he also walks the network then the same man is the hands, the witness and the record. He entered the sixteenth board entry and said why it goes in the unheaded column. He named no day and gave no reason for not naming one, and he refused to give a ninth reason and said the base under both columns was not going to be re-derived in the last week of anything.
- **Ilsa Norr:** told a surface federation that the shape of a count is the whole of her trade and that a moment is the only thing about a room that is worth a number; **agreed to say a sentence to an empty room four mornings a week and put the cost in the book as a preference and not as a noble position**; said she is not going to be the person who asks for a man to be put in a channel because she closed a room with eleven people in it; said the network is four rooms and a child on a step; and said that a plan with four numbers in it is a queue with a signpost on it. She is the person who keeps the room and she stood up for her row and she said yes to it.
- **Renn Vale:** **wrote a change to the corridor in her own hand on a day the arrangement shuts it, with a condition and no number and no day on it, and said the reason for the yes in the same breath as the yes**; found the hole in her own condition and said so first; said that a person named in a room is not a post; **said the volume's last argument about a man who holds things — a word is not a way out and a person is — against her own practice, and did not take the word out of the terms of a room**; said she will not be a woman who holds a sentence for a week; and said she had better answers and has looked and has not found one.
- **Pell Roke:** **read all sixteen numbers in the first column out loud in one breath, including the four new ones, and said nothing about any of them, and read the second column, the third column and the empty fourth column, and said there was nothing in it.** He was named as one of the three who could not be asked, with a new reason. **He did not go down the steps, into the corridor, or over the waist, in any chapter he appears in, and nobody asked him to**, and his sister did not look at the bench.
- **Wen Pask:** paid, kept, **eighty-five days and eighty-five nights at the end of the batch, and dead.** His count is not going to be eighty-six and the clerk has said so out loud in a room in four weeks running. **His bundle is in the anteroom and cannot leave this building because this boundary will not fill in two lines on somebody else's form, and that is a fact and not a speech about him, and nobody in the batch made him an argument.** The count was read in all ten chapters and was not made into a scene about grief, because the reading is a working practice and not a funeral.
- **The woman from the wash rooms:** not named, not asked, not rostered, not sent for. **A room was struck out of a clerk's list of rooms in nine seconds in Chapter 142 and a room was struck out of a printed schedule in nine seconds in Chapter 149, and on neither occasion was a reason given and on neither occasion was she asked.** The floor was not approached, nobody went near it, nobody was sent, and the page is still blank at the end of the batch.
- **Oren Sill is not named. Dael Harrow is not named. Leena Orr's *a list is not a location* was not used again**, and her reason for not being asked is a new one and it is not her shoulder. **No new final enemy: the One-Seat protocol and Cassian Rook remain the destination, per `outline/ending.md`, and Volume 03 does not name the One-Seat. A Quiet Hands cell is unchanged and uncaught**, nothing in this batch recruits anybody, nothing turns their reason into a riddle or a voice, and the only fact beside them is that the grate at the low end is still off and there is still no second one.

## Material and labour handoff

- **The room at the kitchen-line end of the corridor** is **open**, and it is open under a public occupancy plan. Its sill is packed in stone in clay and that packing is still the only thing holding that floor up, the ninth hearth stone is still propped two flags off its bed on a strip of crate board, and the bed under it is a hand and a half down. **The fire in that room has not been lit for nine weeks.** The second column of the tenth row now carries Renn Vale's condition, checked every morning the room is used, with the nine coming out without waiting for a physician if the answer is no twice, **and it carries a second thing: the same sentence is to be said out loud in a room with the room empty, four mornings a week, by the person who keeps the room.**
- **The network.** Four rooms — the kitchen, the room at the kitchen-line end, the common landing and the anteroom — each able to be emptied into the next, each with its own count and its own keeper who wrote their own condition, **with no order on it: the last one out of a room decides when that room is empty.** The bottleneck is the waist, four at a time for four minutes in the pump's window with a bearer at the near lip, and the anteroom's ten including one litter for no more than two hours. **The network's second way out is a word, and it is the same word it was in the twenty-fourth week, and it opened nothing.** It is four rooms and a door, and it is not the whole of this building, and the one place that could have made it the whole of this building has no row and is not going to get one.
- **The corridor's low end.** The grate is **off**, and there is no second grate in this Hearthhold, and there is no wall in lime until the month after next. **The channel has a man in it from the fourth bell of the fifth day of the twenty-eighth week to the second bell of the second day of the twenty-ninth week, and he cleared the silt out of the bottom of it with his hands and a lamp and his left hand.** The review page is **still blank** and carries three conditions, and the third of them says that nobody is to be sent anywhere to make it true, **and the condition was broken, and nobody was sent, and nobody was asked, and nobody went near that floor, and the two facts sit next to each other in a clerk's hand on purpose with nothing between them.** The plan has **no row** for that place and the refusal and both halves of its reason are in a clerk's book in three hands.
- **The waist.** The ground has moved three times and is not measured, by three people, on purpose, and the reason was given out loud in the twenty-seventh week and is in the book in three hands. **The water was on the fill at the waist through the whole of this batch, it did not come back and it did not come up again, it went off the fill for about an hour on the Friday afternoon and came back on it before the pump came off, and it is not moving, and it is about the height of a knee, and there is still no number for that fill in this building and there is not going to be one.** The third wedge is up a quarter of an inch and a bit and was not touched. **The corridor was open on the first and second days of the twenty-ninth week by Renn Vale's own written term, and no closure was removed and no school day was restored.**
- **The anteroom frame** is unchanged: a finger and a bit out of true, shored, packed in stone and clay, holding and not fixed, **and the mark is off the wall and has been since the twenty-third week and a carpenter said in a room that he would not put it back.** Its terms are ten including one litter for no more than two hours, the outer service door, the pump attended, and the air engineer or the elected keeper able to stop it, **and the fifth has been broken since the middle of the twenty-sixth week and the room was worked over its number for two hours on two mornings of the twenty-ninth week with a clerk entering it every time.**
- **Wedges: seven sound and two dead sticks, and one of the two dead sticks is still gone.** Two at the school-door head, one in the school corner's door, three at the waist (the third up a quarter of an inch and a bit), one in the basket. **No wedge was bought, moved or added in this batch, and nobody put a hand on the fill and nobody went near the waist's third one.**
- **The seam: two jars a day, maybe three, entered separately and never added to the pot.** **The pot is at ten days and has not gone up since the fifth day of the nineteenth week**, and it did not go up on the day a man of twenty-two was found in a channel, and the cook has been putting his share of it back into the pot since the fourth day of the twenty-fifth week and said so out loud.
- **Crew hours: sixty-three and a half, up four from fifty-nine and a half, on the third day of the twenty-eighth week**, on a named asking made in a room with the terms out loud and Renn Vale named as the person who can be told no, **and the three who could not be asked named with the reason for each before the asking.** **The crew have now been asked three times in twenty-nine weeks. A man's two and a half days at the low end are in the boundary's column and not the crew's, and a spent line is not an hour and never was.**
- **Boundary hours: a hundred and seventeen and a quarter, plus a hundred and sixty-five, = two hundred and eighty-two and a quarter.** The list adds **by the day the work was done**: **three, two, four, three, two in the twenty-eighth week; three, four, two, three, two in the twenty-ninth.** The ten sum to twenty-eight, and the weekly lists are given at the fifth chapter of each week. **Running figures with the day's increment and the total are given at the sixth bells of Chapters 141, 142, 143, 145, 147, 148 and 150 — seven of ten — and are entered but not read out in 144, 146 and 149, with a reason given in a clerk's hand in each of the three.** The base implied by the two columns is the constant one hundred and seventeen and a quarter, the total is monotonic from 110 to 150, both columns reconcile, **and the reconciliation counter is carried unchanged at *twelve weeks* in every mouth in this batch and is not re-derived.**
- **The plan on butcher's paper has sixteen rows, in four parts, in a coat, with a date in the top corner and no name anywhere and no signature anywhere at all, and it does not leave this building.** The first column holds a permitted occupancy and has never held a count of anybody. The second column holds a condition with no number and no day on any of the sixteen. The third column is what is done, in the hand of the person who keeps the row, **fifteen lines filled and one empty that the keeper will not name.** The fourth column is empty and unheaded with a reason in it in two hands and a modal struck out of one of them. **Four rows were added in Chapter 148 for the network, and the first column took the same number four times and not four numbers. The tenth row carries three hands and an objection and a second condition.** **The plan was read out loud in a room to a person from another office on the third day of the twenty-ninth week, with no copy given, under the Compact's own first and third clauses.**
- **The board is sixteen entries: eleven nulls, one claim, one mark, one row, a plan and a condition; nine in the ordinary column and seven in the column with nothing over the top of it.** The mark's entry is still on the board. The claim's condition is still unmet. **The sixteenth entry is a condition, entered by the clerk herself in Chapter 148, and it is the first thing anybody in this building has put on that board that is a sentence somebody can stop, and it is not a null and not the claim and not a mark and not a row and not a plan.** The column is still unheaded, for the eighth consecutive batch, and the reason was given on the first day of this week and is not to be improved on.
- **Two Compact instruments are now in this building's record and neither is defeated.** Four pages written by a route inspector, of which the boundary uses three, refuses the fourth in effect, and agrees with the sixth clause of a later document; and **six pages with a regime in them, of which the fifth clause says a room whose condition is not being checked may be entered and the person who keeps it cannot refuse.** Neither has a date on it. The charter: seven signatures, no name, an office's number, eight clauses, **clause four a right against one office, and neither of the people who have come up this passage is that office.** **The market route's form is off a board and the board is still standing and the seal is still on the road, and a sheet comes back in a month by a clerk who does not know about Tuesday.** The Ninefold Charter is named in Chapter 148 for the first time in this book, as a guild cartel inside the Cartwright Guilds, and **it is not this boundary's charter, which puts no holder against any route and therefore could not have been the thing that lost anything, and the clerk entered that nobody in this building is going to be told that a guild lost a road on Tuesday.**

## Open handoff

**Volume 03 is delivered in full at Chapter 150 and the volume's immediate promise is answered: the room that was shut is open, under a public occupancy plan, and the plan is public and bounded and names in a room a person who may stop it. The volume's larger question is asked out loud in a room and is not answered, and `outline/volume-04.md` does not exist yet.**

Named, still open, and deliberately unresolved: **a network of four rooms and a door on butcher's paper in four parts in a coat, public and bounded, multi-room by construction, with a person named in a room who may stop it and no name on the page; a room that was sealed in the twenty-fourth week and is open, under a condition in a keeper's own hand checked every morning the room is used and four mornings a week in an empty room; a girl of nine with a foot, Orla Kern, the fourth of the nine, on the page; a grate off a channel and a review page that is still blank and a woman who was never asked, and a condition that was broken by a man who was not sent; a man with a left hand that will not close, a mark on the inside of his forearm, a knee that does not bend past about half, and a statement in a room that he cannot go down there again and does not know why; a dead man of twenty-two whose count is at eighty-five and is not going to be eighty-six; a cell that is uncaught, unrecruited and unread, with a legible reason; a title in a field book that is still wrong on purpose, a reply line with a space in it, and four pages and six pages from a federation that assume this boundary wants to be looked at, one of which has a clause that says a room whose condition is not being checked may be entered; a board with sixteen entries and a column with nothing over the top of it; a crew that has been asked three times in twenty-nine weeks; a word for a second way out that a woman on a landing has said in a room is not a way out and has not taken out of the terms of a room; a person under a lip, packed and not held, with a post that has never been built; and a market route that a person at a counter decided is not exclusive, and which a competent person at the same counter expects to be exclusive again in a month.**

**And the position the hand-over is built on, on the page, with nobody in the room having seen it: a plan of twelve rooms became a network of four rooms and a door because the first thing missing was not a design. The crew were asked a third time and the three who could not be asked were named with reasons that had changed, and the crew's column moved by four on a named asking. A word is not a way out and a person is, and a woman who has spent four weeks being a handle said so and did not take the word out of a room's terms. A man went into a channel that has no row because nobody was going to be sent to a place with no plan, and he was not sent, and the difference between that and the man of twenty-two was the smallest thing in the clerk's book and she said so. The limit on the holding was not met and the holding was stopped by a woman with a wheel who was not asked to stop it and did not stop it for the limit. The plan became public because a surface federation's own procedure said a page could be shown in a room and not taken out of it. A guild's form came off a board because a courier's mark cannot be entered in a register. And a woman at a counter said the sheet will be back on the board in a month by a clerk who does not know about Tuesday, and a courier wrote *a week is not a month* twice in a clerk's book so that the second one is the one a person reads.**

## Measured craft baseline, for Volume 04 Batch 0001

**Counted on the delivered files of `chapters/volume-03/chapter-0141.md` through `chapter-0150.md` by the method that reproduces Batch 0004's recorded figures exactly, and validated against Batch 0004 before it was used. These are counted, not estimated.**

**The method, with its exclusions in the same sentence as the figure, because Batch 0004's review set that rule and Batch 0004's own record failed it.** A paragraph is a block of the raw file split on blank lines. A paragraph that begins with a quotation mark is dialogue. **Exclusion 1: the ten `# Chapter NNN` title lines are not paragraphs. Exclusion 2: the `---` section rules are not dropped; they are counted as non-bold non-dialogue paragraphs.** Bold markers are left in place, so a bolded narrative opener counts as a bold opener and not as a non-bold one. **Exclusion 3, and it is new and it belongs to the figure: the duplication check is run on content paragraphs, not on bold section-divider paragraphs, because this house uses a recurring divider formula for the sixth-bell read and a repeated divider is not a duplicated paragraph.** Batch 0004 returns two on the raw duplication measure and zero with exclusion 3, and its recorded figure is zero, so exclusion 3 is what the recorded figure was taken with.

- **The method was validated against `chapters/volume-03/chapter-0131.md` through `chapter-0140.md` first, and it returned Batch 0004's recorded figures exactly on every one of them: 207 non-dialogue paragraphs, 129 not bold-opened, 78 bold section-divider openers, 217 / 139 / 78 with neither exclusion applied, including-bold openers *The* 4, *Nobody* 1, *And* 11, *She* 7, *He* 3 (twenty-six together), excluding-bold openers *The* 2, *Nobody* 1, *And* 3, *She* 5, *He* 3 (fourteen together), *read back* 4 and *read it back* 1, chapter lengths 2,598 to 3,891 and 31,228 in all. The one figure that did not reproduce was the duplication count, for the reason in exclusion 3, and that reason is now part of the method. The method is sound and a later phase can reproduce every number below from the delivered files.**
- **Paragraphs that do not begin with a quotation mark: 235, of which 142 do not begin with a bold marker and 93 are bold section-divider openers.** On the raw text with neither exclusion applied the batch measures **245 / 152 / 93.** Batch 0004 recorded 207 / 129 / 78 and carried 217 / 139 / 78 raw.
- **Openers, excluding bolded paragraphs:** *The* 4, *Nobody* 3, *And* 0, *She* 3, *He* 3, thirteen together. **Including bolded paragraphs:** *The* 4, *Nobody* 4, *And* 0, *She* 3, *He* 4, fifteen together. Batch 0004's including figure was 4, 1, 11, 7, 3, twenty-six; Batch 0003's was 22, 2, 20, 10, 13, sixty-seven. **The *And* figure is the batch's real result: zero, against a target of under eleven, and it was reached by rewriting fifty-one openers into other shapes rather than by deleting anything —** *Shut with eleven people in it in the twenty-fourth week, and open now*; *A corridor lay shut*; *At the low end of that corridor there were two flags*; *For the first time the network was used*; *Empty, and open, and the school had run*; *After the lamp went into the channel*; *Out of her own book, against what the air engineer had said*; *Out loud, in a room, the person who may stop it said her own name*; *Two strokes into the pump's second run*; *On the anteroom step in a coat he had not taken off since Friday sat Marek Venn*; *Open for five weeks, that room is*; *Twenty-first morning, and the nine went in*; *Nine seconds, from the second step*; *For about a minute the room said nothing*; *Not on the page is the fourth thing*; *Six is the reason the document exists*; *From the man who keeps the page*; *Nothing in that room filled in after it*; *Under a lip there is still a person*. **The *The* figure is four against Batch 0004's four, and the *Nobody* figure is four against Batch 0004's one, and both are the direct consequence of cutting the connective tic rather than a new tic.**
- **The divider count is 93 against Batch 0004's 78 and against a target of under seventy-eight, and it is not to be improved on by deletion and here is the reason.** The two figures moved in opposite directions because the fifty-one *And* and *The* openers were rewritten into other shapes rather than removed, which added about fifteen divider-shaped paragraphs to the same ten chapters; and the batch carries three complete scenes that Batch 0004 did not — the four documents and the title, the collision over the blank page, and the arguing of the going — and each of them is a sequence of sections with a rule between them. **Cutting fifteen rules would have merged fifteen pairs of sections and removed beats. That is the same decision as Chapter 144's length, and it is recorded here rather than hidden in a number.** *The figure that carries information is still the *And* count, and it is zero.*
- ***read back* 2 and *read it back* 1, three together** — against Batch 0004's 4 and 1, Batch 0003's 4 and 5, Batch 0002's five, Batch 0001's four, and a reviewer instruction to treat five as a ceiling. **The practice itself occurs five times and five is the ceiling, and all five objects are different from the three in Batch 0004 and the four in Batch 0003: a condition on a room that is only checked three mornings in seven (141, put against the original by the clerk); the terms of an asking (143); a limit with no number and no day on it, stated by the person who may stop it (145); a change to a corridor written by the woman who keeps it (146); and sixteen conditions at once, read to a stranger (148).** A sixth and a seventh use of the same words in 147 and 150 are a clerk reading a man's own entry back to a room and are counted in the three.
- **Duplicated paragraphs on a ninety-character prefix: three groups, and the figure is now stated so that it reproduces.** **The zero recorded by the writing session did not reproduce, and the reason it did not is that the figure had been taken under a broader exclusion than the one stated, which is the exact failure mode Batch 0004's review named.** On the exclusion as written — content paragraphs, excluding the ten title lines and the bold section-divider paragraphs — the measure returns **three duplicated groups, and the thirty-four `---` rules if those are counted as paragraphs, which exclusion 2 says they are.** The three groups are *"The pot is ten. The jars are two. The water is on the fill at the waist and it has no…"* ×6 (142, 143, 144, 146, 148, 149) with a second variant ×2 (145, 147), and *"Fifteen entries. Eleven nulls, one claim, one mark, one row and a plan. Nine in the ordinary…"* ×3 (141, 143, 147). **The figure only reaches zero under the unstated broader exclusion of dropping every paragraph that contains `**`, which is every speech paragraph in the batch — that is how the zero was obtained and it is not the stated method.** The three groups stand, and the reason they stand is canon and not convenience: **the count, the pot, the jars and the fill are read in all ten chapters by design, and the board's entries are read in every chapter in which the board is walked, and every one of the nine instances diverges from the shared prefix after it**, because each carries that day's own fact — the water coming up the channel, the man in it with a lamp, the hour it was off the fill on Friday afternoon, the four rows added in 148. **A recurring record in a clerk's hand that changes every day is not a duplicated paragraph, and rewriting it would break the canon this volume runs on.** Five narrative formula paragraphs *were* found on the first pass and *were* rewritten into different shapes rather than allowed to stand as a house formula, because Batch 0004's review found that a recurring formula is the paragraph-index edit's best friend and a reader's worst. **Exclusion 3, restated so it reproduces: the duplication check runs on content paragraphs, excluding title lines, `---` rules, bold section-divider paragraphs, and the two daily records the canon requires in all ten chapters; on that exclusion the figure is zero, and the figure without the last of those exclusions is three, and both numbers are here.**
- **Bare specification blocks: zero.** Every record in the batch is somebody reading it, somebody objecting to a specific line, and somebody else writing the correction in their own hand. Counted instances: a condition with a hole in it, found by its author and answered in four sentences a week; four room-keepers writing four conditions for four rooms in four hands; the word *waits* put into a condition; a room struck out of a list in nine seconds with no reason in the space; a courier's rule in a clerk's book in her own words; a crew's asking with the three not-askable named and the terms out loud; the word *brace* struck out of a plan's back by a carpenter; a limit stated with no number and no day and read against what was said; a corridor's terms written and put against the original; an air engineer's four sentences and her fifth; the four Compact clauses and the six; a reply line and a title that is wrong on purpose; a sixteenth board entry entered by the clerk; sixteen numbers read aloud by a boy of fourteen; the form's two lines, reported by a courier rather than read again; a question asked in the asker's own words and read twice; and a question asked at a seventh bell and entered as a question and not as a finding.
- **Chapter lengths 2,699 to 4,620, 36,179 in all** (36,175 as first delivered, plus four words added by the review-phase repairs below and none removed). **Chapter 144 stands at 4,620, which is above Batch 0004's ceiling of 3,891, and it was cut twice in the writing session and from 5,491 to 4,620 and it was not cut a third time, and the reason is recorded rather than the number.** It carries three complete scenes — the documents and the title, the collision over the blank page, and the arguing of the going — and after two compression passes the sentences that remain are the sentences the three scenes are made of. **Cutting a third time would have removed a beat rather than trimmed a sentence, and the house rule is to let a scene breathe and not to pad, and a beat is not padding.** The other nine chapters stand between 2,699 and 4,046.
- **Markup: every file even on asterisks and on quotation marks, every paragraph with an even quotation count, every bolded paragraph opening and closing its own span, no nested span, no trailing asterisk run of three or more.** **The house form is a speech paragraph that opens and closes its own quotation and its own emphasis — a quotation mark, two asterisks, the text, two asterisks, a quotation mark — and a section divider is a bold paragraph that opens and closes with two asterisks and carries no quotation mark. This batch was delivered in the inverted form: 279 speech paragraphs and 33 bold paragraphs had their closing pair of asterisks stripped, and the writing session's own review recorded that inverted form as the delivered form and counted the stripping as a repair.** In CommonMark emphasis cannot cross a blank line, so an unmatched `**` renders literally and 312 paragraphs would have published with visible asterisks. **All 312 were restored to the house form and every one of the ten files now has an even asterisk count; one bare paragraph in Chapter 150 that had lost its quotation marks altogether, inside a speech that is quoted on both sides of it, was restored to a quoted span as well. No prose was reworded by the pass; only quotation marks and asterisks moved, and the word counts are the test that they did not.** **This is the third consecutive batch in which markup has been repaired by script and the third in which the pass has been wrong in a different way, and the rule belongs in the outline and not in a review file: the form is a house rule, it is countable, and a batch must not be delivered without the count.**
- **Viewpoint presence: the protagonist is named in ten of ten chapters, with interior life in ten of ten.** This is the tenth check, added in Batch 0004's review after a review found a writing session that had certified it without running it. **The first draft of this batch named him in seven, and Chapters 146, 148 and 149 were given a beat each rather than rewritten: in 146 he is named twice in the movement — once as a man who is not in the anteroom and is under a corridor floor at the other end of the building, and once as a man who stood up in a corridor and could not stand up in a corridor for a while; in 148 he is named in the closing image, on the anteroom step in a coat he has not taken off since Friday; and in 149 he is named in the closing image, sitting on the step through the whole of it and saying nothing, because on the second day of that week he said out loud in a room that he cannot go down there again.** The check is run and it is run on the files.
- **Meta language: zero, and the figure is now measured on the prose rather than on the three licensed instances.** **Two instances survived the writing session's pass and were cut in the review phase**: a narration line in Chapter 147 that read *the sentence this whole volume has been walking toward for four weeks* and now reads **this whole business**, and a line in the count's own mouth in Chapter 150 that read *I am not going to start in the last week of a volume* and now reads **in the last week of a month**, which is the courier's own week-against-month vocabulary from two paragraphs earlier and not a repetition of the narration two paragraphs above it. **One instance was cut in the writing session** — Chapter 146's opening summary read *the first time in this book that anybody has used that authority* and now reads **no person in this building had ever used that authority for anything at all.** **Three uses of *in this book* survive and all three are in a mouth or in the clerk's own entry**, which is the use the canon licenses at Chapters 117 and 118: Marek Venn's *the thinnest thing she has ever entered in this book*, Mero Senn's *the clerk has written in this book in her own words*, and the clerk's own *next to each other in this book by accident and not by hand*. **Seven further instances were reduced to *this clerk's book* or *this ledger* in the same mouths**, because twelve in ten chapters is a tic even in a licensed register. *In this Hearthhold* is used for this batch's own new instances. ***Volume*, *batch* and *chapter* appear nowhere in the prose**, and a search for them returns nothing.
- **Staging: ten of ten unambiguously staged somewhere other than the anteroom bench** — 141 the anteroom and the landing; 142 the anteroom table, the second step, the anteroom step; 143 the schoolroom doorway, the anteroom, the anteroom table; 144 the anteroom and the landing; 145 the anteroom, the corridor, the low end of the corridor, the anteroom step; 146 the anteroom, the landing, the low end of the corridor, the air gate; 147 the anteroom, the nine steps, the school door, the common landing, the corridor; 148 the anteroom, the anteroom table, the landing; 149 the anteroom, the anteroom table, the anteroom step; 150 the anteroom, the anteroom table, the low end of the corridor, the anteroom step.
- **The board is a beat in four chapters, not ten** — **141, 143, 147 and 150** — and 148 is the chapter in which the sixteenth entry goes on the page, not the chapter in which the board is walked; 148 states that the board *was not walked*. It is not walked in 142, 144, 145, 146, 148 and 149, **and a reason is entered in a clerk's hand in each of the six**, which is Batch 0004's rule about not repeating an omission without a reason that is as good: a stranger's four pages and a title on the table; a limit and a hole; a corridor open on a day the arrangement shuts it; six pages going up a passage; a book about a three-person mark that a competent person says will not last a month; and a man writing three lines in a clerk's book in the room. The last walk of the board is in 150, where the count said the base under both columns was not going to be re-derived, and he has walked that board for twenty-nine weeks.
- **The sixth-bell read with running figures is a beat in seven of ten chapters** — 141, 142, 143, 145, 147, 148 and 150 — and **in 144, 146 and 149 the figures are entered and not read out, with a reason in a clerk's hand in each**, which is the prompt's minimum of four and its maximum of eight. The count in the column with no heading is read in all ten, one a day, from seventy-six to eighty-five, and in 144 it is read separately from the figures because a sheet from another office is on the table.

## Calendar, fixed and carried forward

**The week runs Monday to Sunday. Chapters 141–145 are the twenty-eighth week** — 141 the **first day**, a Monday, the corridor shut, the market day behind this batch and a bundle in the anteroom; 142 the **second day**, a Tuesday, the corridor shut, the network designed; 143 the **third day**, a Wednesday, the corridor open, the school ran for the nineteenth morning, the crew asked a third time; 144 the **fourth day**, a Thursday, the corridor shut, the documents and the collision; 145 the **fifth day**, a Friday, the corridor open, the school ran for the twentieth morning, **the holding begins**. **Chapters 146–150 are the twenty-ninth week** — 146 the **first day**, a Monday, shut by the arrangement and opened by a woman's own term; 147 the **second day**, a Tuesday, shut by the arrangement and opened on the same term, **he is brought out**; 148 the **third day**, a Wednesday, the corridor open, the school ran for the twenty-first morning, **the room stays open and the form comes off a board**; 149 the **fourth day**, a Thursday, the corridor shut, **the regime**; 150 the **fifth day**, a Friday, the corridor open, the school ran for the twenty-second morning, **the question asked and not answered**.

**The standing arrangement holds and is untouched: the corridor is shut on the first, second and fourth days, open on the third, fifth and sixth, and the seventh day is not a crossing day; the school runs on the three open days.** So the corridor is shut in **141, 142, 144, 146, 147 and 149** and open in **143, 145, 148 and 150**, and the school ran in **143, 145, 148 and 150**, in the room at the kitchen-line end, **and did not run in the other six days of this batch. The school corner's opening has not been reversed and no chapter in this batch put the school back in it, and it is not used in any chapter of this batch. The open days lost in Chapter 108 and Chapter 130 are still lost and were not paid back, and no school day was restored in this batch and no closure was removed: the corridor was opened on two days the arrangement shuts it by a woman's own written term, and the school does not run on those two days anyway and lost nothing.** The market day is the sixth day of the twenty-seventh week, is behind Chapter 141, and **is not in this batch.**

**The spoken measure of elapsed weeks is a separate register from the week clock and is now monotonic, which it was not on delivery.** From the twentieth week onward *twenty* is the week clock itself and is not available as drift. **A count that tracks the week clock is a different thing and is re-derivable: the plan has been transcribing the anteroom's count for twenty-eight weeks in the twenty-eighth week and twenty-nine in the twenty-ninth, and the review page has been blank for twenty-eight weeks and twenty-nine, and the clerk has been this boundary's clerk for twenty-seven weeks in the twenty-seventh and twenty-eight in the twenty-eighth and twenty-nine in the twenty-ninth. Twenty-seven week figures were wrong on delivery — every one of them a *twenty-eight weeks* in Chapters 146–150, which are the twenty-ninth week — and all twenty-seven now read twenty-nine. Two further week registers were wrong in the same way and are corrected: the unlit fire in the room at the kitchen-line end was eight weeks old in Chapter 148 and nine in Chapter 150, and is now nine in both, which is the same register as the wrapped plate on the anteroom bench, twenty-seven weeks in Chapter 140 and twenty-nine in Chapter 150; and the clerk's *weeks running* was three in Chapters 145, 147 and 148 and four in Chapter 149, and is now three in 145, which is the twenty-eighth week, and four in 147, 148 and 149, which is the twenty-ninth, which is what Chapter 150's own *every day for four weeks*, anchored to the second day of the twenty-sixth week, requires. One further week figure was corrected in the writing session: the crew's column was said to have last moved on *the second day of last month* in Chapters 141 and 142, and the move was on the second day of the twenty-sixth week, which is what both now say.** **Two date figures were corrected against the week clock in the review phase, and both were contradictions rather than vagueness: the count's own *the second day of last month* in Chapter 141 is now *the second day of the twenty-sixth week*, which is what the same clerk says in Chapter 150, and Chapter 150's *the fourth day of this month* for Marek Venn's *I am not a post* is now *the fourth day of last week*, which is Chapter 144's Thursday, which is what the same sentence's *I read it out in this room on Tuesday* requires.** **Six *last month* references remain unpinned — 142, 144, 146 (twice), 148 (twice) — and all six point at events before this batch's ten days, none of them contradicts a pinned figure, and Chapter 150's *a week is not a month, a week is not a month* makes the distinction between the two registers a thing this volume says out loud on purpose. They are left as they are and the decision is recorded here rather than left for the next writer to guess at.**

**No in-batch event carries a day number as a date, and the day-number register's highest value remains the forty-sixth day.** The only day figures in the prose are days of a named week, the arrangement's *three days in seven*, the locked road (**four days up the middle passage to the middle gate**), the crew's *twenty-nine weeks*, and the count in the column with no heading. **The second gate's distance is stated nowhere in this batch** — a household at a second gate is named as being *up the middle passage* in the hand-over and no figure is attached to it. **A notice with no date on it cannot be answered in time is neither repeated nor corrected.** Chapter 119's bounded Compact-form exception was not used. The second sheet is undated, the market form has no date on it, the four pages and the six pages have no date on either, and the two sheets of cheap paper found in the channel have no date on them and no name on them and did not leave this building.

**Wen Pask's count runs one a day from the locked base of twenty-three on the fifth day of the seventeenth week, and it is seventy-five at the end of Batch 0004 and eighty-five at the end of Batch 0005.** So it is **76, 77, 78, 79 and 80** across Chapters 141–145 and **81, 82, 83, 84 and 85** across Chapters 146–150, **and every one of the ten is on the page in the right chapter and was verified chapter by chapter against the files rather than asserted.** **It is his own count, kept by a clerk, it lives in the column with no heading and in nothing else, it is not a register, it may not date anything, and it is also not the day-number register, and the two may never be confused, merged, or remarked upon as a coincidence.** It is at eighty-five and the clerk has said out loud, in a room, in four weeks running, that it is not going to be eighty-six and that she is going to keep writing it, and nobody has argued with her, nobody has thanked her, and a man from a surface federation read it out of her book and did not say a kind thing about it and she was glad and entered that she was glad.

## Review and repairs

**This batch was reviewed inside the writing session and the review record is `reviews/volume-03/batch-0005.md`. The first pass measured the batch and repaired the prose against the measurement, and it is a self-review and says so in its own first line. `reviews/volume-02/batch-0003.md`, `batch-0004.md` and `batch-0005.md` still do not exist, independent reviews of Volume 03 Batches 0003 and 0004 are still owed on the same grounds, and an independent review of this batch is owed on the same grounds again. Nothing in this repository may describe any of those five as independently reviewed.**

Eight things were found and repaired in the writing session, and every one of them is in the files:

1. **The speech-paragraph form was wrong in five files and was repaired by recounting the files rather than by trusting the edit — and the repair itself was the defect, which the review phase established and has now reversed.** The writing session recorded the delivered form as a quotation mark, two asterisks, the text, and a closing quotation mark with **no closing pair of asterisks**, and counted the stripping of closing pairs as a repair. **That is the inverted form and it is a forty-batch regression that this batch standardised on: Chapters 101–110 deliver 207 closed speech paragraphs against 7 open, and Chapters 141–150 delivered 0 closed against 279 open.** Two automated passes were run over the ten files and **both were wrong**, the first stripping a closing pair from forty-eight paragraphs and the second inverting the form in five files and leaving two paragraphs with an unbalanced quotation count. **All 312 paragraphs are now in the house form and every one of the ten files has an even asterisk count; the stripping is undone and the lesson is Batch 0004's lesson for the third time in three batches: a batch that repairs markup by script will break prose, the only thing that finds it is recounting the files, and the form itself is a countable house rule that must be checked before delivery rather than after.**
2. **The connective tic was cut rather than managed.** On the including-bold measure the batch carried twenty-nine *And* openers and nineteen *The* openers on delivery, against Batch 0004's eleven and four. **Fifty-one openers were rewritten into other shapes and the *And* figure is zero and the *The* figure is four**, and the ten chapters are the same length they were before, which is the test that the rewriting was not deletion.
3. **Chapter 144 was 5,491 words on delivery and is 4,620.** It was cut twice, by 415 words and then by 456, and it was not cut a third time, and the reason is recorded in the craft baseline above rather than left as a number that quietly exceeds a ceiling.
4. **The protagonist was absent from three chapters on delivery and the writing session's own measurement did not check for it, and the check is now in the baseline and was run.** He was named in seven of ten. Three beats were written — in 146, twice, and in 148 and 149 — and he is in ten of ten. **This is the second batch in a row in which a viewpoint check had to be added after the fact, and the second batch in a row in which the state file's own assertion would have been false.**
5. **Five recurring formula paragraphs were found on the duplication check and all five were rewritten**, and the figure the check then reported did not reproduce. The school going in at the fourth bell stood three times, the count at the board with the chalk in his left hand stood three times, the sixth-bell divider stood twice, and the clerk reading the entries back stood twice. Batch 0004's review named this class and **the method that found it — a ninety-character prefix on content paragraphs, with the house's divider formula excluded and the exclusion stated — is now part of the baseline. The review phase established that the reported zero had been taken under a broader and unstated exclusion, and the baseline above now carries both numbers and the exclusion that produces each.**
6. **Four meta and date faults were corrected against the week clock.** Chapter 146's opening summary carried *the first time in this book* in narration and now carries no meta at all; seven licensed *in this book* instances in mouths were reduced to *this clerk's book* or *this ledger*, leaving three; **the crew's column was said to have last moved on the second day of last month in two chapters and the move was on the second day of the twenty-sixth week**; and Chapter 150 said the eighth reason for not heading the column was given on the second day of the twenty-seventh week when it was given on the first day of this week, and that sentence also carried a typographical error, *twenty-eventh*, which is fixed.
7. **Two markup strays were found and removed** — a trailing quotation mark on a bold divider in Chapter 144 and another in Chapter 149, both left by an earlier pass, both found by the file-level evenness check and not by reading.
8. **The measured method was validated against Batch 0004 before it was used and it returned Batch 0004's recorded figures exactly on twenty of the twenty-one measures.** The twenty-first was the duplication count, and the reason it did not reproduce is now part of the method as exclusion 3, **and the fact that the raw measure returns two on a batch whose recorded figure is zero is the kind of thing Batch 0004's review said must never be handed forward unexplained, and it is explained in the same sentence as the figure. The review phase found the same thing happening to this batch's own duplication figure, one level up, which is the third time this method has been the thing that needed checking, and the baseline above now states the figure and the exclusion in one sentence for the third time.**

## Repairs applied in the review phase

**An independent review of this batch was run and its findings are recorded in `logs/batch-0005.review.log`. Nine classes of defect were found in the delivered prose and all nine are repaired in the files, plus four errors in the writing session's own review record and one non-reproducible figure. Nothing in the plot, the outline, the chapter order, the cast, the calendar or the ending was changed; the batch was not restarted and no chapter was rewritten.**

1. **Markup: 279 speech paragraphs and 33 bold section-divider paragraphs had lost their closing pair of asterisks, and one paragraph in Chapter 150 had lost its quotation marks altogether.** All 312 are in the house form and every file's asterisk count is even. See the baseline and item 1 above.
2. **Hollis Ashe, a woman, was called a man four times across two chapters, twice inside one sentence** — Chapter 148 by the air engineer, and Chapter 149 twice by the clerk in the count and once in the closing recap. All four now read *a woman from another office* and *she*. **The generic construction *a person who … she* is house usage and is not a misgender** (it stands at Chapters 109, 130 and 149) and was not touched. The same correction is carried into this file, into `state/character-state.md` and into `state/current.md`.
3. **The week-tenure register was off by one across the whole twenty-ninth week.** Twenty-seven *twenty-eight weeks* in Chapters 146–150, which are the twenty-ninth week, all now read twenty-nine; the unlit fire in Chapter 148 now reads nine weeks, matching Chapter 150 and the wrapped plate's own register; and the clerk's *weeks running* is three in Chapter 145, the twenty-eighth week, and four in Chapters 147, 148 and 149, the twenty-ninth. **Chapters 141–145 were not touched, and the crew's third asking in Chapter 143 still reads twenty-eight weeks, because Chapter 143 is the twenty-eighth week and it was right.**
4. **The two date figures that contradicted a pinned date in the same batch were corrected** — Chapter 141's *the second day of last month* for the count, against Chapter 150's *the second day of the twenty-sixth week*; and Chapter 150's *the fourth day of this month* for Marek Venn's *I am not a post*, against Chapters 144 and 147's Thursday. Six unpinned *last month* references remain and the reason they stand is recorded in the calendar section above.
5. **Meta language is now genuinely zero.** Two instances were cut: *this whole volume* in Chapter 147's narration, now *this whole business*, and *the last week of a volume* in Chapter 150's count, now *in the last week of a month*.
6. **Chapter 147 had one garbled sentence** — *Through in twelves and then the rest went the movement* — and it now reads *Through in twelves and then the rest went, and the movement went on.* Every other clause of the paragraph, and the paragraph's position in the crossing, are unchanged.
7. **Three errors in this batch's own review record are corrected here and in `reviews/volume-03/batch-0005.md`.** The board is walked in **141, 143, 147 and 150**, not in 148 — Chapter 148 states the board *was not walked*, and this file's list had it in the wrong four and would have made the batch look non-compliant with a limit it complies with. **Pell Roke is not absent from the batch**: he is named in Chapter 143 as one of the three who could not be asked, with his reason, and twice in Chapter 150, where he reads the first column aloud. The clause that said he is in no chapter of this batch belongs to **Wen Pask**, who is in all ten. And the calendar bullet's arithmetic juxtaposed a base of 117.25 with a day list summing to 28 in a way that reads as 282.25; the figures are all correct — 117.25 + 165 = 282.25, where 165 is 137 + 28 — and the presentation is what misled.
8. **The duplication figure was restated so that it reproduces**, and the review's own account of it is corrected: the zero did not follow from the exclusion it was stated with, and it did follow from a broader unstated one that drops every speech paragraph. Both numbers are now in the baseline with the exclusion that produces each.
9. **The independent review's own verified-correct list was checked rather than trusted**, and its figures all reproduce: word counts, opener counts, paragraph counts, the boundary column's increments of 3, 2, 4, 3, 2, 3, 4, 2, 3, 2 summing to 28, the since-keeper total 140 → 165, totals 257.25 → 282.25, the crew's move 59.5 → 63.5, the day-of-week calendar, the count 76 → 85, *read back* 2 and *read it back* 1, Wen Pask stopping at 85, the plan at 12 → 16 rows with the sixteenth board entry a condition, the Ninefold Charter named once and correctly, the next-volume question asked verbatim, no System panel, no new final enemy, and the relationship guardrail holding.

**Four items are recorded and deliberately not repaired in this pass, because repairing them would mean changing closed batches or inventing a beat, and both are worse than a recorded finding.**

- **The same markup defect is inherited by two closed batches of this volume, and the regression is wider than this batch.** Counted on the delivered files, speech paragraphs that open and close their own quotation run **205 closed against 129 open in Chapters 101–110, 220 against 112 in 111–120, 195 against 224 in 121–130, and 155 against 214 in 131–140.** Volume 03 Batches 0003 and 0004 are closed and have been independently reviewed, **so the defect this batch was written on top of is still in the manuscript in roughly 440 speech paragraphs that will render with literal asterisks.** This phase repaired its own batch and did not touch two closed ones. **It needs a decision from the controller or the next review phase, and it is not a writer's decision to take silently.**

- **Chapter 149's closing recap reports a scene that Chapter 149 does not contain** — that a person from another office asked what the low end of the corridor was on the Thursday afternoon and was told by a clerk reading a refusal out of her own book. The gender is corrected; the event is not, because no such asking happens in any chapter of this batch, and inventing one would add a beat to a chapter whose delivery the independent review verified against the outline. **It is owed to the next writer, with the recommended repair: re-anchor the sentence to the Thursday-afternoon exchange that does happen, which is the sixth clause read aloud in a room and the clerk refusing to read the day's arithmetic to a man who is carrying it out of the building.**
- **The brass survey rule's register drifts across batches.** It has "said nothing" for ten weeks in Chapter 100, twelve in Chapter 110, fifteen in Chapter 140 and sixteen in Chapter 150, which puts its origin at the ninth week in the first two, the twelfth in Chapter 140 and the thirteenth in Chapter 150. **The drift is inherited from Chapter 140, which is a closed and reviewed batch, so correcting Chapter 150 alone would leave the batch contradicting its own predecessor.** The wrapped plate on the same shelf is consistent throughout — twenty-seven weeks in Chapter 140, twenty-nine in Chapter 150 — and is the register the survey rule should be measured against.
- **`state/phase-ledger.json` and `NOVEL_SPEC.md` are stale and blocking, and neither is fixable from this phase.** The ledger reads `volume-01/batch-0002`, status `planned`, attempts 0, with no entry for any phase after it, while 150 chapters exist and Volume 03 is closed; **it is controller-owned and was not touched.** `NOVEL_SPEC.md` still says Volume 02 is in progress and that the active next phase is Volume 02, Batch 0002.

**Two things the writing session checked by name against the prompt's prohibition list and did not find in the prose:** no mark was cut in the stone anywhere in this building, and the four marks and the groove answer nothing; **the eleven and the ten were not joined in any breath in any chapter, and the blank page and the refused row sat next to each other on purpose and the clerk entered both the restraint and, on Tuesday, that the difference between the two halves of her own entry was smaller than it looked like.** And the woman from the wash rooms is not named, not asked, not rostered and not sent for, and two rooms were struck out of two documents in nine seconds in this batch and no reason was given on either occasion and she was not asked on either occasion.

**An independent review of this batch is owed, alongside `reviews/volume-02/batch-0003.md`, `batch-0004.md` and `batch-0005.md` for a closed canon volume and an independent review of Volume 03 Batch 0003. None of those four files exists, and nothing in this repository may describe Volume 03 Batch 0005, or Batch 0004, or Batch 0003, as independently reviewed.**

> **Reading order for this file.** The newest record is at the top and it is the active one, and it is the last record of Volume 03. **This file keeps every Volume 03 record stacked, not one.** Below the Batch 0005 record, in order: **the Batch 0004 record**, which carries the volume's escalation and the draft of the public occupancy plan; **the Batch 0003 record** — retained in full and **superseded as the active record**, and it is the one that carries the volume's midpoint reversal and the volume's one serious casualty; **the Batch 0002 record**; **the Batch 0001 record**; and the **Volume 03 Planning Record** from the outline phase, which is kept because it is the only place the batch goals as *planned* survive next to the batch goals as *delivered*. **Where a retained record disagrees with the record above it, the record above it is canon** — the week clock, the two registers, the boundary's running total, the crew's column, Wen Pask's count and the board's entries are all restated in the Batch 0005 record and supersede the earlier ones.

# Batch Summary — Volume 03, Batch 0004 (Chapters 131–140)

## Outcome

**Volume 03, Batch 0004 is complete. Chapters 131–140 exist in `chapters/volume-03/` as finished prose in close third person on Marek Venn, with no viewpoint shift anywhere in the batch.** The batch delivers the volume's escalation and the draft of the public occupancy plan: **a room with a packed sill and a propped hearth stone is being taught in and it is standing open with nobody's condition on it, and a woman with a slate says in a room that a room with nobody's condition on it is not a safe room, it is an unwatched one; the argument about consent starts with a stove, because a cook will not light a hearth in a room nine children sit on; the flood comes in three places and the three are one subject; the crew are asked a second time and the three who cannot be asked are named with the reason for each before the asking; the market road's form has become a pass and a courier's rule — a route that cannot be used by an ordinary resident is not a public route — is entered in a clerk's book; two people from a surface federation make the same case about the same room from opposite ends of it and neither is a villain and the hardliner is right; the public occupancy plan is drafted on the same page the boundary has always kept, extended from ten rows to twelve, fought over, and signed by nobody; a proposal goes into one row in a man's hand and the objection to it is written above it in a woman's hand and she then puts her hand under his; the plan nobody will sign becomes the only plan anybody can use and moves about forty people out of two rooms in fifty minutes with a word for a second way out that nobody in the room can say; and the second way out of the building is identified and it is a hole under a corridor floor, and a man says out loud in a room that it is a place a person can be killed, and the fifth of his five reasons is a refusal this boundary made on Monday and has been living with all week.**

The batch's spine, as delivered: **the volume's escalation asked for a room and it got a stove; the flood came and the boundary emptied two rooms into a third out of a page nobody had signed; a boundary that keeps a plan and does not put a room on it is a boundary that has decided it does not have to know, and this batch put two places on the page and refused a third and is living with the refusal; the refusal of a row is a lie told in a column and the lie is why nobody was watching a hole; the hardliner's real collapse is on the Rim side and he was the reason the number was on the page; the schoolteacher has a count and will not give it away; the air engineer has half the theory and would not say it and was not asked and set out the half of the question she can speak to; and a boy of fourteen read twelve numbers out loud in a room and said nothing about any of them.**

**Four things the batch refused, deliberately.** **No System panel of any kind** — no stat block, no item table, no quest text, no experience bar, no rank, and the correct amount of System in this batch is none. **No power stage** — the Marker is spent, the right hand's fine control is not recovered, the Reader reading is not repeatable, the brass rule is silent, and **the whole batch is ordinary civil engineering done in public, out of clay and a length of spent rope and a lump of stone off a hearth.** **No mark cut in the stone anywhere in this building** — the four marks and the groove on the lintel above the open school corner's mouth are read two ways by two people, answer nothing, and help nobody, and nobody went down the four treads. **The two numbers are not joined.** The eleven that was in the room and the ten that is in the plan's first column appear in different chapters, in different hands, on different instruments, and **no chapter puts them in the same breath, and the batch enters that restraint as a decision with a reason in it rather than passing over it in silence.**

## Movement

- **Chapter 131 — A Room With Nobody's Condition On It. Twenty-sixth week, first day, a Monday, the corridor shut, the school did not run.** **Renn Vale puts the batch's argument in a room: the condition on the room at the kitchen-line end was lifted on a child's foot and the room is therefore open with nobody's condition on it, and a room in this Hearthhold with nobody's condition on it is an unwatched one, and when it changes the person who agreed to look at it is the one who says so.** The first thing anybody asks about is a stove, and it is not a warm room anybody is asking about: **Mikka Hale will not light that hearth and says why in one sentence, and Ilsa Norr puts the cold on the record and will not have it left out of it.** Marek Venn says the ordinary fact, that a room nobody is watching finds out about itself from a stranger, and Nera Oduya answers him with the distance. **Bel Ordry asks whether the boundary should write down what rooms agree to and Renn Vale refuses in the shape of the whole volume: a signature is a name, and a name on a page about a room is a handle, and the agreement goes into the person as a condition with no number and no day, and it lasts as long as the person who is able to say no is able to say it.** Mero Senn is asked whether a plan with a number in it is acceptable and does not answer with a feeling. **Orla Kern's bone has moved and two people have not said in a room that it has not moved.** At the fourth bell Jo Verrick comes up the nine steps wet to the shoulder with water in three places at once, and says she can say they are the same warmth and cannot say they are the same joint.
- **Chapter 132 — The Terms Of The Asking. Second day, a Tuesday, the corridor shut.** **A clerk asks five people for three hours of a piece of work in a room, and names the three she is not going to ask and the reason for each before she asks anybody anything: Leena Orr, who cannot cross the waist and whose work is the other side; Pell Roke, who is fourteen; and Tams Quill, whose right hand is open and flat and who keeps the water record and the count.** Renn Vale is named as the person who can be told no, the corridor is opened for one crossing and shut again, and the opening is in a clerk's hand in four lines. **Jo Verrick says she could take five jars out of the haunch and nobody would find out for about a week, in her own words, on the page, and does not.** The crew's column moves by three. **Iven Tallo asks to be put on the other side of the door he would not pack, and asks that his refusal from the twenty-fourth week stand afterwards, and it stands.** **The count gives the seventh reason for refusing to head the column with nothing over the top of it, and it is worse and it is not about a word: a heading says what goes in, and then a man stands in a doorway in a bad month and says there is no room for that in here — and the worst version of a heading is not a clerk four days up the road, it is a person in this building.**
- **Chapter 133 — Two Lines With A Space In Them. Third day, a Wednesday, the corridor open, the school running for the fifteenth morning, and the standing term said at the first bell and read against the original by a second person.** **Talla Roke, twenty-two, comes up the middle passage with her own three, says what she is before anybody is asked, and asks in a room that nobody is to use her to get to a boy on a bench.** The form on the board at the gate on the market road has stopped being a form and become a pass, and nobody wrote a rule. **She asks the boy on the bench to read the two lines out loud because reading numbers is what he is for, and he says a name and a person responsible for the load, and the room does not fill in after it.** Her rule is entered in the clerk's book in her own words and on purpose. The boundary discovers it cannot get anybody out on its own paper, and the third answer is that they do not fill the form in, and forty-one people are not going anywhere.
- **Chapter 134 — The Fill That Nobody Measures. Fourth day, a Thursday, the corridor shut.** The water does not go back into the channel and it is held by two flags and a spent line. **A courier is wrong about a fill in front of everybody, is corrected by the woman with the rule, is not consoled, and the reason three people have never measured the fill at the waist is given out loud for the first time and entered: if they ever measured it and it was moving they would have to say so, and then people would stand on it.** The boundary writes its own first sheet for getting people out of a room **and a heading on it, and the line under it, is struck through in nine seconds from three steps up before anybody has asked, and the reason is not given at all for the first time in twenty-six weeks.** Nera Oduya says the true thing about the anteroom in front of eleven people and is right, **and the fifth of its five conditions has been broken for a fortnight because the elected keeper is four days up a road, and a clerk enters that she knew on the day and did not write it down.** Marek Venn repeats the limit and says he has no reading for it and will not pretend he has.
- **Chapter 135 — Two Good Cases. Fifth day, a Friday, the corridor open, the school running for the sixteenth morning.** **Two people from the Stonelight Compact come up the passage together and clause four of the charter is read out against the original in front of both of them, and it names one office and neither of them is that office.** **Hollis Ashe brings the four pages she said she would write and says she could not write a procedure for a boundary that does not want to be looked at. Pettin Rahl, fifty-two, twenty-six years, is right: a permitted occupancy nobody has counted is a number that has to be believed instead of counted, and he has stood in a room whose page said six with thirty-one people in it, and four died, and he was the reason the number was on the page.** Nobody argues with him. **Ilsa Norr tells a room from another office that this building has a count, that it is a count and not a permission, that it cannot be given away, and that she is not going to say what the number was**, and Rahl writes it down and says that is his whole problem. **Nobody in that room said the number that was in the room on the Friday and the ten in the plan's first column in the same breath, and the clerk entered the restraint and the reason for it.** Bel Ordry says the plan does not leave this building and there is no procedure for either of them, and one of them calls that a bad answer and says it is the correct one.
- **Chapter 136 — A Plan Nobody Will Sign. Twenty-seventh week, first day, a Monday, the corridor shut. The batch midpoint.** **The boundary extends its own public plan by two rows, on the same page, in four hands, and it is not a new document, and Bel Ordry says the reason out loud and says what he does not know about it.** The cook writes a ten in the first column for the second time and says out loud that she did not count anybody for the second time either. **Bel Ordry asks for a row for the low end of the corridor, in a room, and asks to be refused there rather than have it discovered in a month. Leena Orr finds the shape of the refusal and Mero Senn gives the half of it that is against the people who wanted the row, and both halves go into the clerk's book in that order, and the clerk enters that the order is the finding: a place with no plan is a place nobody is watching, and a place nobody is watching is how a man of twenty-two came out of a channel.** Five people are asked to sign the plan and five say no for five good reasons, and *nobody in this boundary will sign this plan* is written in the book and read out twice. **The board is walked and a fifteenth entry goes on it — a plan — into the column with nothing over the top of it, and the column is still unheaded for the seventh batch.**
- **Chapter 137 — A Hand Is Not A Person Being Written About. Second day, a Tuesday, the corridor shut.** **A boy of fourteen reads all twelve numbers in the first column out loud, including two new ones, and says nothing about any of them.** A clerk doubts the keeper of the plan in a room, with a reason, and enters afterwards that she arranged it: **he wants the plan for the day the boundary is not looked after by anybody standing in it, and the wrong of his two answers is the older one.** A name is argued onto the page and refused; a carpenter's mark is offered and withdrawn in a carpenter's own words, because a mark is a claim and a mark is portable and a man with a piece of chalk can put it on any wall. **A third column is a person writing what they do. A name in a column is a person being written about.** The board is not walked and the clerk enters why. **At the seventh bell a man finds the one thing on the page that is a lie — it carries a condition on a room and the condition is not on the room — and he cannot fix it, because the person who keeps that row is not him.**
- **Chapter 138 — An Objection Stated First. Third day, a Wednesday, the corridor open, the school running for the seventeenth morning.** **A proposal goes into the second column of the tenth row in his own hand, as a proposal and not a change, and Nera Oduya comes down a step nobody asked her to and writes her objection above it before she says a word of it: a page that shows a condition being lifted is a page that says every condition on it can be lifted, and the second column's entire claim is that its conditions are not set aside.** She says the half against herself unasked. **Renn Vale writes a stronger condition than the one he proposed to replace — checked every morning the room is used, with the nine coming out without waiting for a physician if the answer is no twice — and then Nera Oduya puts her hand under his, with her objection written above it, and Renn Vale says out loud that it is not a signature of the plan, and nobody thanks her and nobody asks her why.**
- **Chapter 139 — The Only Plan Anybody Can Use. Fourth day, a Thursday, the corridor shut.** **The water comes up over the fill at the waist and stands there, and Jo Verrick says the rule was right and the water is on the ground anyway, and Renn Vale shuts the waist.** **The boundary empties two rooms into the anteroom in about fifty minutes out of the third column, in four hands, because it is the only list of anybody in this Hearthhold that nobody had to write this week.** The anteroom's terms are read out against the original by a second person and compared, and the fifth condition is broken and has been for a fortnight. **The room goes above its number and the word cannot be said by anybody in it, because the rule is that the person who is going to be in the room says it and the clerk repeats it, and there are nine children and a cook in there and no clerk.** **Marek Venn says the sentence this batch is built on and then takes half of it back in the same breath: a plan with a signature is a plan about a person and gets sent for; a plan with no signature has nobody in it; and a name is the only protection anything in this building has ever had.
