The batch's spine, as delivered: **the school corner's floor is wet and the water is the boundary's own drinking water; three candidate rooms are refused in the right hands and the boundary ends the day with no room; nine children cross a fill that has moved three times and stay on the far side; the school corner is shut as a room — a correct, cheap closure that closes no corridor on any day of the week and strands nobody; a competent Stonelight Compact inspector arrives that the charter does not cover and the boundary has to invent a procedure for a body it never wrote one for; the keeper goes up four days of road and the boundary runs the rest of the batch without him; a second sheet amends the charter to name a fourth object of care; nine register boxes and a shelf's worth of school cross the waist and one of the three open days is lost against a closure added; a competent guild office closes a water-lock with four households behind it; and the plan's first column is read aloud and the boundary finds out it has been writing down a nine-hundred-year-old permission instead of a population.**

**Three things the batch refused, deliberately.** **The Marker stage is not in it** — it is owed at Chapter 112 — and Chapter 109 refuses it out loud, in a room, with the clerk entering that she did not understand the second half of it. **The keeper's promise is not kept in it** — that is the twenty-second week and Batch 0002's — and Chapter 105 stages only the departure and Chapter 106 reads the two true sentences out in a room. **There is no scene on the road and no scene at a counter**, and therefore no viewpoint shift.

## Movement

- **Chapter 101 — Under The School Floor. Twentieth week, first day, a Monday, the corridor shut.** The only reason the school corner has an empty morning in it in twenty weeks is that the corridor is shut, and **Ilsa Norr finds the water at the second bell** — the low corner, the flag dished a finger and a half to the door — and says the sentence the next nine days are about: *a schoolroom floor is a floor and I have been standing on the answer since the eleventh week.* The finding is staged in the room's order, found, located, tested, recorded, and not before. **Renn Vale gives the wall away in one sentence: a wear surface is a use.** Jo Verrick locates a bed joint under the floor that runs six feet along the party wall to the haunch, and the air engineer says no in public — a course of dressed stone is what a course of dressed stone is for — and is **wrong**, and Jo finds a stone out of that course and a stone back in it in lime, and **the course is not continuous**, which is a fact about this building nobody in it knew. The clerk writes *from* and the air engineer strikes it for *in a joint that runs to the haunch*, because *from* asserts a cause and the page has a place. **Marek Venn has the answer in his mouth — cap it — and does not say it**, because a cap of clay takes the only drinking water this boundary has found with it when it lets go, and because the jars are the cook's separate numbers and not the pot, so the pot would not go up, it would stop being topped, and Mikka Hale would find that out at the fifth bell and not from him. He says *the floor is the problem and not the water* instead, and the cost is named: the school does not run here tomorrow either. **He gets nothing off the haunch for the twelfth time and the board goes to twelve entries — eleven nulls and one claim, nine in the ordinary column and three in the column with no heading.**
- **Chapter 102 — Two Rooms. Second day, a Tuesday, the corridor shut.** Three candidate rooms and all three refused, in the refusals' own words, and none of the refusals is a veto. **Sefu Aran refuses the anteroom standing up, on purpose, because a keeper who refuses a room from the wheel has made the wheel into the room's terms**, and the air engineer refuses it on air: a room whose second way out is a word is a room whose only ventilation is a sentence, and on the day she is not here it is nine children and one door. **Ilsa Norr empties her own school store herself**, in the shape she emptied it in the thirteenth week, and refuses it: *the school is not going into a store*, and a room you teach in is a room you sit on the record in. The two parts of the plan come out of the store into Bel Ordry's coat, and he says out loud that a coat is a worse place than a locked store. **Renn Vale stops the conversation twenty feet short of the third room**, in the same shape she stopped a party in the eleventh week, and the refusal goes into the ledger in four lines with no name against it and nothing struck out of it, and the floor is not approached. **Marek offers the crew's hours and is stopped a second time, by Renn Vale, with the sentence that the column is a quantity of asking** — it moves when a person in a room asks the five of them for a work, with the terms out loud and a person named — and nobody has asked. **The crew's hours do not move.**
- **Chapter 103 — The Last Walk. Third day, a Wednesday, the corridor open.** The school does not run in the school corner at all this week, and nine children and a schoolteacher go down the corridor. The crossing is staged the way a crossing is staged in this building: four at a time, at a walk, in the pump's strokes and not in the six minutes, a bearer at the near lip, two readers, the plinth reader's word a warning and not a stop. **Renn Vale says out loud that she is not going to put her hand on the fill, and says it before she does not**, and gives the reason: a person with a hand on a fill has made a decision that the ground is fine, and a person with a hand on a fill keeps their hand there. Isel Ferrow stops a party at the waist for eleven seconds so that a person can say out loud that nobody has touched the third wedge. The air engineer comes down four steps and gives the air rule, and the fifth child goes with a bearer who is not her mother. **Two parties and a fifth, nine minutes and forty seconds, entered; the terms say four minutes, and the terms were written for six pails a day and not for nine children, and the room is told it has to decide which to break.** Leena Orr is at the clinic on the far side of the waist and is not asked to help and says one thing about who gets carried back. **Pell Roke reads the plan's first column aloud at the fifth bell from the bench at the top of the nine steps and does not go over the waist.** Ilsa Norr's condition goes on the page in her own words on one line: the school is on the far side of a fill from the clinic and from me, and I am not going to pretend I have agreed with that, and I am going to make it happen anyway, and both of those are true. **And Renn Vale says one sentence nobody answers: there is a way to shut that room.**
- **Chapter 104 — The Cheapest Answer. Fourth day, a Thursday, the corridor shut.** **Renn Vale proposes the closure, and she has had it on her slate already**, and Dalla Sen enters that a woman who arrives with a thing written down has been thinking about it when nobody was watching her. The cook objects to the object, not the closure: *say what shutting it does* — and the answer is **nothing to the water**, because shutting a door does not touch a bed joint, and **it was never for the water; it is for the floor.** Renn Vale asks the room to find an objection and **not one of them does**. Marek asks the question nobody asked — *what did we just stop being able to do* — and gets two answers that are not good: Renn's is that the school has three days in seven on a fill and three days in seven nowhere; **Ilsa Norr's is that the boundary has stopped being able to be wrong in a room with children in it, and she is not defending the room, she is defending the argument.** They shut it with a door and a wedge and nothing bought, and **Jo Verrick states that one of the three wedges at the school-door head has gone into it, so the school door has two where it had three and nobody is behind that door to be pushed against anything**, and it is a move and not a use. The condition goes on Renn Vale's slate with **no day on it and a person who can stop it and a person who can be stopped**, and the clerk keeps it on a separate page so that the two pages disagree on purpose. At the fifth bell two people read the marks on the lintel above the shut mouth and read them differently, and neither says, and **nobody cuts a mark in the stone.** **The arrangement is untouched — shut on the first, second and fourth, open on the third, fifth and sixth, school on the three open days — and the clerk enters that this closure is a room and closes no corridor day, with the reason, so that in six weeks nobody has to argue it again.** Marek writes one line in his own book: *a room kept safe by being a room nobody is in is a thing this building now knows how to do, and I do not think anybody above the Cinder Steps has worked out yet that this is cheap.*
- **Chapter 105 — The Line Above The Steps. Fifth day, a Friday, the corridor open. The batch midpoint.** **Hollis Ashe**, forty-one, sixteen years, Stonelight Compact route inspection, is in the anteroom at the second bell and nobody in this building saw her come in. Her permit covers a line of stations on the surface above the Cinder Steps and the approach to it, **and it does not cover this, and she says so before she asks anything.** **Clause four binds the Cartwright office and does not cover her, and the boundary has no procedure, and Bel Ordry says that out loud in the room.** The four sentences are **argued, not printed** — Renn Vale on the first (*a rule that lets this boundary say no is a rule a stranger can use to shut a room; put the other half in, or the sheet is a door with manners on it*), Renn again on the second (*two is wrong in the way that gets people hurt; a document that says a thing is refused is worth more up a road than a document that shows a page*), Dalla Sen on the fourth (*we cannot keep this, and I would rather write it and be caught keeping it than leave it out and be caught having thought about it*), Mikka Hale on the third (*there is a man in this building who has spent twenty weeks keeping a page in a coat so that a stranger could not carry it out of a room, and I want him in the room for this sentence*). She asks to see the plan, gets the third column **written out in words in a clerk's hand, and the third column leaves the building** — the first thing this Hearthhold has ever sent a stranger that a stranger could file. She asks what the anteroom's second way out is and is told **a word**, and writes it down, **and Marek understands four seconds too late that he has just described a rule that can be handed to somebody, and he does not warn the room, and it happened about four feet in front of nine people who did not see it either.** She asks about the stair and the boundary says *not a route and not in anything* and she writes it down. She asks, in a room, **who is the person that answers for the school on the waist**, and there are eleven seconds of silence, and **Marek knows the answer and does not say it, because the second he says it that arrangement becomes an office**, and Renn Vale answers the question that was actually asked and says that there is no person, and the inspector writes down that a worse answer than a name is a worse answer than a name. She asks whether to write *refused* and the room says **write *not covered***, and she tells them what that costs her own office. **The discovery lands at the sixth bell: there is a chalked inspection line above the Cinder Steps and it has been there for nine seasons, and this boundary has been having its argument about the middle gate's pails under inspection the whole time.** Marek tells Bel Ordry to his face what he is — *a man who can be found is a man who can be checked, and a man who cannot be found is a man who has to be trusted* — and Bel Ordry does not defend it and does not take it back. **And at the last bell the elected keeper says he is going up the middle passage to keep a promise, and gives the reason, and the room does not argue with him and does not thank him, and nobody says when he gets there.**
- **Chapter 106 — The Wheel Goes With The Keeper. Twenty-first week, first day, a Monday, the corridor shut.** **The boundary discovers that it has been carrying the wheel in four other places for twenty weeks and had not noticed.** The pump runs attended, the sixth minutes come and go with the post on the review page still blank, the pot is read at the fifth bell by a woman who was not waiting for anybody, and **nothing goes wrong, and that is the finding, and by the fourth bell the room is in a bad mood about it.** **The keeper's two true sentences are read out at the third bell, standing, in the anteroom**, because he left them with the clerk to be read to whoever would have to act on them — *nothing comes, this boundary is not sending anybody, and the four households have their own water and did not ask this boundary for any of it; and this boundary has found two jars a day out of its own wall and is using them and is not sending them, and the true sentence is that it looked after itself while four people carried water up four sets of steps, and those two things happened at the same time and one of them was this boundary's choice.* The cook asks for them to be read at the fifth bell every day. **Renn Vale decides that they stand and are read and decides nothing else**, because a room that decides what to do about four households' water is a room that has started sending somebody. **Marek Venn goes and finds out what would happen if the keeper never came back, and finds three things that want a keeper's word, and all three have a substitute, and not one substitute is a keeper.** He says out loud that he has wanted to be needed for twenty weeks and has been needed twice, and both times for a floor and a wet joint and a woman with a rule. At the seventh bell he writes the **first entry in his own book that is about him and not about a plan**: *a room can hear the true thing about itself and go on to the fifth bell, and that is the boundary working and not the boundary being all right.*
- **Chapter 107 — The Second Sheet. Second day, a Tuesday, the corridor shut, the keeper on the road.** Four sentences lie face up on the anteroom table where anybody can read them, **and the clerk chose that and says so and will not let anybody else bring it up first.** The charter names three objects of care — a stair, a lip and a valve — and the boundary now has a fourth, a wall with water in it. **Two positions are argued in full before anybody marks anything**: Renn Vale's (*adding a fourth thing is putting a claim on a wall, and the whole of what this boundary has done for nineteen weeks is refuse to make claims*) and the cook's (*leave it out and there is no answer on the page, and the moment the fourth is in somebody's mouth it belongs to that person and will be said in a different tone on a bad day*). **The tie is a person and the person is the air engineer and the cook, and it is two hands and not one.** Bel Ordry cannot sign, because the second signature below the keeper's belongs to a man on a road, and a name taken off is a name somebody can put back. **The air engineer strikes the woman's covers out of the second sheet in nine seconds and says the reason out loud instead of writing it**, and nobody in the room can produce one sentence in which anybody has ever asked her. **The price is named by somebody who is not the clerk: two documents that do not say the same thing can be split, and a split is a person in the middle picking the one that suits him.** The sheet is written in two hands with a date in the top corner and no name at the top of it and **clause nine untouched**. **Iven Tallo is not asked to sign it, notices, and says something about the first charter and not the second** — and the last of it is that a man who is relieved to be left out of a document in a bad month is a man who has been taught what a relief feels like, and he has taught it. **And at the fifth bell the clerk does the thing the room has owed a man for a month: she asks Bel Ordry for his coat in front of nine people, counts the four parts, makes him say which is which without looking, and then enters that the clerk thinks what he wrote on Friday was very good and that she has no way to check it and has not looked for one, and enters those two sentences in that order.**
- **Chapter 108 — Nine Children In A Numbered Room. Third day, a Wednesday, a corridor-open day this batch closes.** **Renn Vale closes the corridor at the first bell and says the reason before anybody argues** — a fill that has moved three times, nine register boxes, nine children, no second window, because the low end stands in water for the six minutes after the lever comes off twenty-six times a day and the only window is the strokes. **Dalla Sen refuses the room's request to hold the closure and the lost day apart for an hour and says out loud that she is going to put them next to each other with a reason beside them and nothing between them, and that the reason is not going to be a reason.** The school crosses in three parties in the pump's strokes, with Wen Pask carrying boxes and saying nothing about it, and the count column has to be read before each party because the only page in this building with a count in it is the plan — **and the anteroom's number is ten, and the anteroom is empty, and Ilsa Norr refuses to cross on the boundary's arithmetic and counts aloud at both ends instead.** A boy of about eleven asks, in the middle of the dip, at a walk, **where the number comes from**, and the teacher says *we will find out* and **nobody follows it up.** The two beech lengths are the props under the shelf the nine register boxes stand on, and **a carpenter tells the room before he is asked, and then says no when he is asked**, in a craft reason a room can check: a boundary that loses its record in a week is a boundary a stranger can rewrite in a month. The props stay, the school goes without a shelf, the boxes go on the hearth stones. **The school runs two bells and then does not run**, and the trade is entered on the room's own page and not on the plan's, in a schoolteacher's words: *the school is in the worst room in this building and it is dry, and I did not agree with that, and a room shut with a wedge is not the same as a room taken from us.* **The day was lost and nothing was paid back and Ilsa Norr was not told which of the two is the independent one and has not asked.** And at the last bell a courier brings up a paper with **no date on it** that uses the word **attended** — a word this boundary reads four hundred times a day and has never once read the way it is written there.
- **Chapter 109 — Behind A Good Reason. Fourth day, a Thursday, the corridor shut. The batch climax.** A notice from the second gate says the water-lock is **closed for survey and is attended**, and that the office will provide a butt and a hand-pump **at the counter, on the day of the survey only.** **The cook reads that last part back and says what it is doing: the man is behind the door, the water is in front of it, and the four of them are behind the door with the man.** **Renn Vale says it is not their door, that she has been trying two days to find the mistake in it, and that there is not one** — a competent office, on a clearance, for a good reason, with a man in a hut, and a door, and two brothers and their sister and one house that only carries behind it. **The four sentences come off the table and are used for the first time inside the boundary, and Bel Ordry reads them off the clerk's copy, and he drops half of the second sentence and the tail of the third, and Renn Vale notices**, and the two pages agree with each other and the reading did not, and the clerk enters that the clause he dropped is the clause about *what a thing is and not what it is for*, next to the anteroom's rating and the word *door*, because they are the same sentence. **The boundary does the small true things and refuses the large one in the same breath**: the water-carrying is unchanged and nothing extra goes down the passage, the two jars are not sent and the cook says no before she is asked, a copy of the two sentences goes up on a courier's hand, and **Bel Ordry does not put his ring on it and says why — a ring on a sheet is a mark on a sheet, and a mark on a sheet is a thing a person can be found by.** Sending a copy is not keeping the promise, and the room is entered as knowing the difference. **The cook states the cost in her own words and it is the longest line in the clerk's hand in twenty-one weeks: we found two jars a day and kept them, four people are behind a gate, both of those are true at the same time, one of them was this boundary's choice, and I would do it again on Friday.** **Marek Venn asks to go and gives four reasons and cannot**, and the fourth is the one he is ashamed of: to go up that road he would have to be a person somebody at a counter could send for, and this boundary does not put people in registers, and a boundary that does not put people in registers cannot go and get anybody. **And then he refuses the Marker out loud, in a room, with the ordinary means for the frame named and exhausted — no timber, no mortar of the right kind, six sound wedges and two dead sticks, a right hand that will not close — and says there is one thing left that is not a reading and that he is not going to do it, and Dalla Sen enters, in her own hand, that she did not understand the second half of it and is not going to pretend she did.**
- **Chapter 110 — The First Column. Fifth day, a Friday, the corridor open, the school running. The batch aftermath.** The six things are read and then Dalla Sen says **Numbers.** **Pell Roke reads the first column aloud at the fifth bell from the bench at the top of the nine steps**: *ten, four, eight, ten, six, nine, five, two, and a dash.* Nothing fills the space after it. **The check is done in front of the room in chalk against the slate and takes forty minutes and checks three explanations, all of them possible, and the true one is not any of them** — the clerk did not miscopy, and the slate is her hand and the page is the plan's, **and what her hand is on is writing down numbers that are said out loud in front of her, eleven times, and nobody has ever asked her what the numbers are numbers of**; the number is not misheard, it is the most repeated number in this Hearthhold; the room is not miscounted, it has never held under ten and has never held over ten. **Leena Orr stops the room from the bottom of the nine steps, because nobody in this building can shout up nine steps: a list is not a location, and a count is a list, and this page has been calling a permission a population since the day anybody here first picked up a chalk.** The school store is open, which it is only because the register boxes are on hearth stones, and **the anteroom's ten on the dowel is the highest number on the dowel**, and Nera Oduya says one professional sentence and **does not say her theory**, and asks the clerk to enter that the fourth thing bound to *when this boundary is keeping itself* was approached in a room and not met. **Dalla Sen asks her for a piece of work and not an opinion — write down what the second column is for — and it is done in four minutes when it should have taken nine, and the rest of it was not asked for and nobody in that room is going to ask.** And the finding's second half: **a room with nine children in it has no row on the plan at all, and a blank row in a new document is an omission and an omission reads as an accusation, and that is the first time the clerk has understood why the ninth room has no row and it is not because two women are stubborn.** The board is walked twice, no day named, **forty-five days and forty-five nights** in the column with no heading, **still unheaded for the fourth batch running**, and the reason is worse: *a heading is a claim about what a column is for, and the day it can be checked is the first day one of its contents does not fit.* **The crew are at fifty-two and a half and the boundary at a hundred and fifty-three and a quarter, and the count's list of thirty-six adds and both columns reconcile.** And Marek Venn says the last thing in the room in three parts: a page can keep a number after the people have gone and **he is not going to write a theory about it this week, and that is a decision and not modesty**; **we have learned how to keep a room by making it a room nobody is in, the price was a wedge out of a school door, it worked, everybody in that room voted for it, he was the only one who could see it was the first of a series and he did not say so;** and **he does not know the other way and the person who has to work it out is him.** And the batch's last line is his: **a page in a coat is not a system, that is a man, and the man is on a road.**

## Required character, relationship and power change

- **Power: none, and the batch says so out loud.** No bearing mark, no route change, no binding, no relay, no brace, and no repeatable reading. **The Marker stage is refused in Chapter 109**, in a room, with the ordinary means for the anteroom frame named and exhausted, the one remaining operation named as the one thing left, and the refusal made in front of nine people; **Dalla Sen writes down that she did not understand the second half of it.** He says it is a different sentence from *anybody who asks me to read a boundary is going to be told no*, and he is not confusing them. **His right hand's fine control is not recovered** and is on the page as a thing that cannot hold a pencil, a rail, or a five-hour day. **The brass survey rule says nothing for the twelfth week and nobody looks at it.** **The board on surfaces goes from eleven entries to twelve: eleven nulls and one claim, nine in the ordinary column and three in the unheaded one, and the twelfth is a null in his own hand because a woman told him where to put his hand.**
- **The only Cairn mark in the batch is on the lintel above the shut school corner's mouth: four marks, one broken through, one a groove, read two different ways by two people, answering nothing, and no mark cut in the stone by anybody.** **There is no System panel, no stat block, no item table, no quest text, no rank and no experience bar in the batch. The System's silence is correct.**
- **Nera Oduya:** asked for a **piece of work and not an opinion** in Chapter 110, and refused to be hurried into the rest of it; **wrong in public in Chapter 101 and not consoled**, with the correction entered in her own hand beside her own words; **strikes the woman's covers out of the second sheet before anybody has explained it to her**; **does not say her theory**, and her condition is approached in a room and not met, and the clerk enters that nobody asked her and that nobody is going to. **No intimacy, no recognition scene, no acceleration, and no scene whose only content is her and Marek Venn.** Their closest moment is again a working moment in front of other people. **She is not leaving and no chapter stages her going; the narration does not narrate a departure either.**
- **Bel Ordry:** writes the first document in twenty-one weeks for an audience that is not in this building, is told to his face what he is by a man with no standing to tell him and is right, does not take it back, **and is actually doubted out loud** — the clerk demands his coat in front of nine people, makes him say which part is which, and then enters that she has no way to check the thing he wrote on Friday and has not looked for one. He refuses to put his ring on a sheet going up a road, and says why. **A keeper of a page is not a holder of a route is not withdrawn and was not touched.**
- **Sefu Aran:** refuses the anteroom standing up and on purpose, and goes up four days of road at the end of the batch. **He is a boundary's absence that turns out to be a boundary's proof**, and his two true sentences are read in a room in his own building.
- **Ilsa Norr:** finds the water, empties her own school store and refuses it, moves nine children across a fill, **answers the question nobody asked — that the boundary has stopped being able to be wrong in a room with children in it — and is not defending the room**, loses a day of school, **will not have it written as moved or as paid back**, writes down what she gave up, and is not told which of the two things is the independent one and does not ask.
- **Leena Orr, Pell Roke, Tams Quill, Jo Verrick, Mikka Hale, Renn Vale, Dalla Sen, Iven Tallo, Wen Pask:** each has one job and one cost. **Leena Orr's *a list is not a location* is used once, in Chapter 110, from the bottom of nine steps, and it earns the volume's discovery.** **Pell Roke reads the plan's first column aloud at the fifth bell because reading numbers is what he is for, and he does not go down the steps, into the corridor, or over the waist.** **Tams Quill explains why the crew's figure has not moved, puts forty-five days and forty-five nights in a column that is still unheaded, and gives a worse reason than last week's.** **Jo Verrick finds the patch in the course with a lamp and a rule and says the floor is dished and the joint is dished and that is the same fact told twice.** **Wen Pask carries boxes across the waist and says nothing about it, because being asked is a different thing from being thanked.**
- **Iven Tallo is not asked to sign anything, notices that he was not asked, and says something about the first charter and not the second.** He is not a defector, not a martyr, not a saint, and not redeemed.
- **The woman from the wash rooms is not named, not asked, not rostered and not sent for.** The third candidate room is stopped twenty feet short of the sentence, the refusal is entered in four lines with no name against it and nothing struck out, **the floor is not approached, and her covers are struck out of the second sheet in nine seconds.**
- **Oren Sill is not named.** A line refused in the fourteenth week stays refused and is in the unheaded column and is not itemised a third time.
- **Talla Roke is not in the building.** Her sheet is a fact on a road and it carries paper and small goods and no people.
- **Hollis Ashe is competent, bounded and not a villain**, and **her questions are good**: she is right that this boundary is drinking out of a wall it occupies, right that a room whose second way out is a spoken word cannot be shown to a stranger as a second way out, and right that *not covered* will end with her writing a procedure of her own. **She is not the Cartwright office and the charter does not cover her.**

## Material and labour handoff

- **The water's course is now known.** The seam is a lime-mortar joint in the haunch behind the anteroom's outer service door. **The course between the haunch and the school corner's floor is not continuous**: a stone has been taken out of it and put back in lime about a finger wide, and the bed joint runs out of the bottom of the patch, under the school corner's floor, six feet along the party wall, and comes up at the low corner of a floor nine children have worn dished toward a door. **The seam's own threads have not increased. The water is still two jars a day, maybe three, in the cook's separate numbers, and neither jar was sent anywhere.**
- **The pot is at ten days and has not gone up and does not go up in this batch.**
- **The school corner is shut.** A door, a wedge out of the school-door head, nothing bought, eleven minutes. **A new standing condition in Renn Vale's hand, with no day on it, a person who can stop it and a person who can be stopped.** **It is on the slate and not on the plan, and it is a room and not a route: it closes no corridor on any day of the week and the standing arrangement is untouched.**
- **The school has moved to the room at the kitchen-line end.** Dished floor, a cold hearth, no mouth, the best light in the corridor and the worst floor in it, no shelf. **Nine register boxes on the hearth stones. The two beech lengths stayed under the school store shelf. The school corner's register boxes are out of the store, and the store is now the only place the dowel has ever been.**
- **Wedges: seven sound and two dead sticks still, and none added and none bought** — one was moved from the school-door head to the shut room's door head, so the school door has two where it had three. **The third wedge at the waist is up a quarter of an inch and a bit and was not touched.** The ground at the waist has moved three times and is still not measured, by three people, on purpose.
- **Crew hours: fifty-two and a half, unchanged since the fifth day of the nineteenth week, and the reason is now on a page in a room in Renn Vale's words** — the column is a quantity of asking and it moves when a person in a room asks the five of them for a work with the terms out loud and a person named who can be told no. **It was offered twice in this batch and refused twice.**
- **Boundary hours: a hundred and seventeen and a quarter, plus thirty-six, = a hundred and fifty-three and a quarter, and the count's list of thirty-six adds by the day it was done** — four on each of the first four days of the twentieth week, three on the fifth; four on each of the first three days of the twenty-first, three on the fourth, two on the fifth. **The running figures at the sixth bells of 102, 103, 106 and 108 give the day's increment and the total since the keeper signed, and all four are stated.**
- **The column with no heading gains forty-five days and forty-five nights and is still unheaded for the fourth consecutive batch**, with a worse reason: a heading is a claim about what a column is for, and the day it can be checked is the first day one of its contents does not fit.
- **The anteroom frame is unchanged and is now spoken about in a room as a thing with no ordinary means left.** Shored, holding, not fixed, a finger and a bit out of true, and the rating still restored by the word *door* said to a page and repeated by the clerk. **The word *door* and the clause about *what a thing is and not what it is for* are now on the same line of the clerk's book, and the clerk says they are the same sentence.**

## Open handoff

**Volume 03 is open and Batch 0002 is owed at Chapters 111–120, the twenty-second and twenty-third weeks.**

Named, still open, and deliberately unresolved: **the four households at the second gate**, who still have nothing, whose water-lock is closed for a survey by a competent office, and whose keeper is on a road with the true sentence in his coat and **whose promise is not kept until the twenty-second week and is Batch 0002's scene and no other batch's**; **the anteroom frame**, a finger and a bit out of true, holding and not fixed, **with no ordinary means left and one operation refused in a room**; **the shut school corner**, with its condition, its person who can stop it, its wet floor, its shut warm mouth going into the throat, and **four marks and a groove on the lintel that two people read two ways**; **the school in the worst room in this building**, which has no row on the plan at all; **one of the three open days, lost, unpaid, and not to be paid back**; **the charter's hole**, clause four binding one office, with a four-sentence procedure written for a second institution and a third column already gone out of the building in a clerk's hand; **the second sheet**, in two hands, disagreeing with the charter on purpose; **the air valve, asked and not answered, and the spring**; **the air engineer's theory, and the name she said in the twelfth week, and neither of which may be asked for by a room**, with the condition *when this boundary is keeping itself* approached in Chapter 110 and not met; **the ninth line and the four treads and the eighth term**; **the plan's one empty third-column line, which Bel Ordry will not name**; **the waist**, moved three times, wedge up a quarter of an inch, not measured; **the plate**, wrapped, unopened, joined to nothing; **the brass rule**, silent for twelve weeks; **Form 9-C**, unattended; **Form 7-B**, unsigned; **the guild's fourth paragraph and the halfpenny sheet and the three other Hearthholds**; **Iven Tallo's bond and an apprentice off the roll**; **Wen Pask, paid, kept, forty-five days, not a carpenter, and the half hinge that is not his**; and **a person under a lip**, packed and not held.

**And the volume's standing discovery, made in a room in Chapter 110 and not joined to anything by anybody: the plan's first column has been recording a permitted occupancy rather than an actual one, the anteroom's ten has been ten since the fifteenth week, the ten came off a nine-hundred-year-old dowel in a locked school store that nobody in this building has ever had read out to it, and the new classroom has no number at all.**

## Measured craft baseline, for Volume 03 Batch 0002

**Counted on the delivered files of `chapters/volume-03/` by the same method that reproduces Volume 02 Batch 0005's recorded figures — paragraphs that do not begin with a quotation mark, on a 90-character prefix for duplication. These figures were recounted after the independent review and the repairs and are the counted values, not estimates.**

- ***read back* and *read it back*: 3**, against **3** in Batch 0005, 3 in Batch 0004, 4 in Batch 0003, 7 in Batch 0002 and 32 in Batch 0001. **Held.** Counted separately, and as in Batch 0005 the two must not be added together, the wider *read it out* family is **6** in the plain-opened paragraphs and is the real tic if a later batch wants to watch one.
- **Paragraph openings beginning *The …*: 6**, against **6** in Batch 0005, 16 in Batch 0004, 17 in Batch 0003, 33 in Batch 0002 and 46 in Batch 0001. **The class remains the smallest in the batch and single figures hold, but the downward trend did not survive the repair pass and the next batch should be told so: restoring the checkpointed dramatised prose into Chapters 101, 106 and 107 added plain *The …* openings, and thirteen of them were brought back inside the count by bolding section dividers (the book's own convention, which the plain-prefix method does not count) and rewording four openers. Hold it at single figures, and if a later batch finds the count climbing, bold the dividers rather than cut the prose.**
- **Paragraph openings beginning *Nobody …*: 9**, against **8** in Batch 0005, 11 in Batch 0004, 13 in Batch 0003 and 9 in Batch 0002. **Held at or under eleven.** The device is load-bearing in this batch — a silence nobody fills, a question nobody answers, a condition nobody is asked about — and it should stay load-bearing and not become a tic.
- **Paragraph openings beginning *And …*: 6**, against **2** in Batch 0005 and 12 in Batch 0004. **Held under eight, not zero.** One caution for the next batch, and it is the reviewer who gave it: **this book also opens a great many section dividers with a bold *And*, and those are not counted by the plain-prefix method.** Counting openers with the bold markers stripped gives *And* at **39** for this batch, which is the batch's true connective load. **A batch that wants to cut the tic has to cut the bold section dividers, not the plain paragraph openings.**
- **Paragraph openings beginning *She …* (11) or *He …* (14): 25 across the batch, of 297 paragraphs that do not begin with a quotation mark**, against 11 in Batch 0005 and 21 and 22 in the two batches before. **The *She* figure has been lifted from 2 to 8, which is what the previous record asked for**, because this batch has a schoolteacher, a cook, a clerk, an air engineer, two brothers and an inspector in it. She and He together are 24, under thirty.
- **Duplicated paragraphs on a 90-character prefix: zero. Bare specification blocks: zero.** Every record in the batch is somebody reading it, somebody objecting to a specific line, and somebody else writing the correction: the wet floor found, located, tested and recorded with a preposition struck in the air engineer's hand; the crossing's four minutes against nine minutes forty; the closure's condition in Renn Vale's hand and the ledger's reason in the clerk's; the four-sentence procedure argued sentence by sentence with four objections before a mark was made; the second sheet in two hands with a covers clause struck in nine seconds; the ninth register boxes and the trade on a room's own page; the four-sentence procedure read off a copy and dropped, with the two pages compared in a room; the check in chalk against the slate; and the board walked twice with a thirty-six-item list by the day.
- **Chapter lengths 2,425 to 3,996, 32,843 in all. Every chapter is under 4,000 and the longest is 3,996.** The shape is a rise into the midpoint pair (105 and 109), and the repair pass lifted 101, 106 and 107 back up into the same range, so the batch is now 3,635 words longer than it was at first save. **The increase is dramatised prose that was in the checkpoint draft and had been replaced by summary of scenes that no longer existed; it is not padding, and the ceiling is still held at every chapter.**
- **Staging: seven of ten unambiguously staged somewhere other than the anteroom bench** — 101 (the school corner, the party wall, the anteroom step, the haunch), 102 (corridor, school store, kitchen), 103 (corridor, dip, waist, school door, kitchen line), 104 (school corner door, the common landing, the shut mouth), 107 (kitchen, anteroom table, school store), 108 (corridor, school store, kitchen-line room, the waist), 110 (kitchen, school store, the new classroom, the lower level) — against a floor of eight, and the repair pass moved 101 over the line. **106 is still the batch's clearest craft debt and Batch 0002 should hold eight**: the three stations in Marek's round are three corners of the anteroom rather than three rooms, and the fix is to send one of them somewhere with a body in it. **The sixth-bell board read is the batch's punctuation and appears in every chapter, which is one beat too many; a later batch should make it a beat in four chapters and give the other six a different closing instrument.**
- **No bare meta language remains.** Twelve instances of *this chapter*, *this volume*, *this batch*, *a volume ago* and *the reader saw it* were cut in the first repair pass, and the independent review found four more and they are cut too — *the whole of the next nine days is going to be about*, *that is how this room stages everything, and that is how this morning went*, *the reason the second one said nothing is the next chapter*, and *the time is the thing this chapter is for* in Chapter 103. **The batch now refers to days and weeks and to the book only in the register the book already uses.**

## Calendar, fixed and carried forward

**The week runs Monday to Sunday. Chapters 101–105 are the twentieth week** — 101 the first day, a Monday, the corridor shut and the school corner's floor found wet; 102 the second day, a Tuesday, three rooms refused; 103 the third day, a Wednesday, the corridor open and the school crossing the waist for the last time; 104 the fourth day, a Thursday, the closure passed; 105 the fifth day, a Friday, the corridor open, the inspector, and the keeper's departure. **Chapters 106–110 are the twenty-first week** — 106 the first day, a Monday, no keeper in the building; 107 the second day, a Tuesday, the second sheet; 108 the third day, a Wednesday, **a corridor-open day this batch closes**, and one of the three open days lost; 109 the fourth day, a Thursday, the water-lock; 110 the fifth day, a Friday, the corridor open and the first column read aloud.

**The standing arrangement holds and is untouched by anything in this batch: the corridor is shut on the first, second and fourth days, open on the third, fifth and sixth, and the seventh day is not a crossing day. The school runs on the three open days.** So the corridor is shut in **101, 102, 104, 106, 107 and 109** and open in **103, 105, 108 and 110**, and **108 is open-and-closed by this batch.** **The school corner's closure is a room and not a route and changes no corridor day. No later batch may promote it into a route closure and no chapter may put the school back in the school corner.** **One of the three open days is lost in Chapter 108 against a closure added on a day the arrangement had open, nothing is paid back, and Ilsa Norr has not been told which of the two is the independent one and has not asked.**

**The spoken measure of elapsed weeks is a separate register and was not re-derived, not corrected, and not used to date anything.** From the twentieth week onward *twenty* is the week clock itself and is not available as drift. **A count that tracks the week clock is a different thing and is re-derivable: the school corner's mouth has been shut for twenty weeks in the twentieth week, and the plan has been transcribing the anteroom's count for twenty-one weeks, and both are stated in the prose and neither is corrected.**

**No day number is attached to any in-batch event, and the highest value in the day-number register remains the forty-sixth day.** The only date on any object is the date in the top corner of the plan, and that is still the plan's whole distinction from the dowel. **No forward count of days or weeks is put on a courier**: the road is four days up the middle passage to the middle gate and then on past it, **the second gate's distance is not stated anywhere in the batch**, the notice from the second gate has no date on it, the halfpenny sheet is not referred to, and **the batch's standing discovery about a notice with no date on it is neither repeated nor corrected.**

**Wen Pask's count runs one a day from the locked base of twenty-three on the fifth day of the seventeenth week, and it is thirty-five at the end of Volume 02.** So it is **36, 37, 38, 39 and 40** across Chapters 101–105 and **41, 42, 43, 44 and 45** across Chapters 106–110, and every one of the ten is on the page in the right chapter. **It is his own count, kept by a clerk, it lives in the column with no heading and in nothing else, it is not a register, and it may not date anything.** **It is also not the day-number register, and the two may never be confused, merged, or remarked upon as a coincidence.**

---

# Volume 03 Planning Record (Outline Phase, no prose written — superseded by the delivery above)

**This section is retained as a record of what was planned and is superseded by the delivery above. The chapters it describes now exist and the prose is canon wherever the two disagree.** The Volume 03 outline phase produced three files and **no chapters**. It is kept at the head of this file so that the next writer sees the plan before the previous volume's record; the Volume 02 Batch 0005 record is retained in full beneath it and is canon.

## What this phase produced

1. **`outline/volume-02.md`** — the volume outline Volume 02 never had, authored after the volume closed, reconciled against Batches 0001–0005 in a ten-row table and ten numbered divergences. **The largest structural debt in the repository is paid.** Where it and the delivered prose disagree, the prose is canon and neither volume reopens.
2. **`outline/volume-03.md`** — the Volume 03 forward plan, *The Weight of a Door*, Chapters 101–150.
3. **`outline/batches/volume-03-batch-0001.md`** — ten cards for Chapters 101–110.
4. **`workspace/volume-03/batch-0001/PROMPT.md`** — the single next phase, and the only one.

## The batch goal, as planned

**Volume 03 Batch 0001, Chapters 101–110, the twentieth and twenty-first weeks.** Open Volume 03 on the delivered canon, not on the series outline's fragile charter. **The boundary can just about keep itself, which means it has every reason to keep doing the cheap thing, and the cheap thing is a closure.**

The school corner's floor is wet, and the water is the boundary's own seam and the only drinking water it has. Nine children have nowhere to go but across a fill that has moved three times and is not measured, on purpose, by three people. **The school corner is then shut as a room — a correct, cheap, competent closure that costs nothing, strands nobody, closes no corridor on any day of the week, and changes what the boundary is.** A competent Stonelight inspector arrives on a lawful permit from a line above the Cinder Steps **that this boundary did not know was there**, and the charter does not cover her, and the boundary has to invent a procedure for a body it never wrote one for — **and anything it invents is a document a stranger can use.** The keeper goes up four days of road to keep a promise he made out loud, **the boundary runs the rest of the batch without its elected keeper and discovers that it runs perfectly well, and his two true sentences are read out in a room in this building so that the people who would have to act on them know what they are.** The charter is amended by a **second sheet** that names a fourth object of care, because a wall with water in it has joined a stair, a lip and a valve. Nine register boxes and a shelf's worth of school cross the fill into the worst room in the building, carried by nine children and a teacher, and **one of the three open days is lost and a closure is added on a day the arrangement had open.** A competent guild office closes the second gate's water-lock for a survey, and **four households are behind it**, and the boundary's two instruments are both about this building and neither is about four people four days up the road.

**The batch ends with the plan's first column read aloud in a room and the boundary discovering that for twenty-one weeks it has been writing down a nine-hundred-year-old permission instead of a population** — and a room with nine children in it and no number in the plan, **which is a room nobody has promised anything to.**

## Movement, as planned

- **Ch 101, twentieth week, first day, Monday, corridor shut** — the school floor is wet and it is the boundary's own water. Found by a schoolteacher who counts children and not barrels. **Marek gets the twelfth null.** He does not cap the seam — **capping it would stop the only drinking water the boundary has found, the only thing available to cap it with is clay, and clay shrinks, and the jars are the cook's separate numbers and not the pot, so the pot would not even go up, it would stop being topped** — and he knows he is choosing the problem over the answer, and he says so in his own book and nowhere else.
- **Ch 102, second day, Tuesday, corridor shut** — three candidate rooms and all three refused in the right hands, and the third not permitted to be discussed. **The school store's own keeper empties it and refuses it.** Marek offers the crew's hours to move the school and is stopped again, this time by Renn Vale, and the sentence is about the column being a quantity of asking.
- **Ch 103, third day, Wednesday, corridor open, school running** — the last walk. Nine children cross the waist at a walk, two parties and a fifth, with a bearer, two readers, a bell and a stated time, and **Renn Vale does not put a hand on the fill and says so out loud before she does not.** **They stay at the far end and do not go back to the school corner.** The school ends up on the wrong side of the fill from the clinic and from its teacher, and both of them know it and neither says it.
- **Ch 104, fourth day, Thursday, corridor shut** — **the safe closure is proposed by the right person, is correct, is cheap, costs nothing, strands nobody, and passes.** **The school corner is taken out of use as a room and shut: a door, a wedge, and nothing bought. It is a room and not a route and it changes no corridor day.** A new condition on Renn Vale's slate, in her hand, **with a condition and not a date, because her terms do not take a number**, and a person who can stop it, and it goes on the slate and not on the plan. Ilsa Norr's answer to *what did we just stop being able to do* is the one that is not good: they have stopped being able to be wrong in a room with children in it. Marek is the only person in the room who can see that this is the first of a series, and being the only one is a thing he wanted for nineteen weeks and does not want.
- **Ch 105, fifth day, Friday, corridor open** — **the batch midpoint.** Hollis Ashe, Compact route inspector, forty-one, sixteen years, works a line of chalked stations on the surface above the Cinder Steps. **Clause four does not cover her and there is no procedure.** Bel Ordry shows her the third column in words and it leaves the building in a clerk's hand, and **the first thing this Hearthhold has ever sent a stranger that a stranger could file.** She asks what the anteroom's second way out is, is told *a word*, and writes it down — **and Marek understands, too late, that he has described a rule that can be handed to somebody.** She asks, in a room, **who is the person that answers for the school on the waist**, and nobody answers. The keeper says he is going up the road.
- **Ch 106, twenty-first week, first day, Monday, corridor shut** — **a boundary with no elected keeper in it**, and it runs perfectly well, and that is the finding and it is not a comfort. **The whole chapter is in this building in Marek's viewpoint: no road, no counter, no viewpoint shift. The promise is not kept here and Batch 0002 keeps it.** What the chapter does is read the keeper's two true sentences out in a room, because he left them with the clerk to be read to whoever would have to act on them: **nothing comes, this boundary is not sending anybody, this boundary found two jars a day out of its own wall and is not sending them, and this boundary looked after itself while four people carried water up four sets of steps, and both of those were true at the same time and one of them was this boundary's choice.** The room decides they stand and are read at the fifth bell while the keeper is gone, and decides nothing else, because nothing in this building reaches four days up the passage.
- **Ch 107, second day, Tuesday, corridor shut** — the charter names three objects of care and the boundary now has four, and the answer is **a second sheet and not a change**: in two hands, **with no date on it, on Bel Ordry's argument that a dated page is a record of a day and this one says *now***, not reopening a signed instrument, with the price named aloud that **two documents can disagree and a document that can disagree is a document that can be split.** **Iven Tallo is not asked to sign and notices that he was not asked.** The four-sentence procedure for a Compact inspector is written and is **the first document in twenty-one weeks written for an audience that is not in this building.**
- **Ch 108, third day, Wednesday, corridor closed by this batch** — the school is moved, all of it, and **nine children carry their own register boxes across a fill that has moved three times**: nine register boxes and a shelf's worth of school, in the pump's window. **The two beech props stay under the shelf and the school goes without a shelf.** One of the three open days is gone. **The closure and the lost day are on the page next to each other with nothing between them and a reason beside them**, and Ilsa Norr is not told which of the two is independent. One child asks where the number on the page comes from, and the teacher does not answer it, and nobody follows it up.
- **Ch 109, fourth day, Thursday, corridor shut** — **the batch climax.** A competent guild office closes the second gate's water-lock for a survey, on a clearance, correctly. Four households are behind it. **The first toll on a closed door is not money; it is a form.** The boundary sends water and **a copy of the two true sentences in the clerk's hand, as paper**, and **refuses to pretend it can do more**, and sends nobody, and the keeper is on a road and cannot be recalled. **Sending a copy is not keeping the promise; the promise is Batch 0002's, said by a man in person at a counter, and the room is entered as knowing the difference.** Marek wants to go and cannot: a two-foot-eight corridor over eleven feet of shaft, a hand that will not close, a crew that is not on a rota, and nine children on the far side of a fill. **The chapter does not resolve and must not be made to.**
- **Ch 110, fifth day, Friday, corridor open** — **the batch aftermath.** Leena Orr stops the room with *a list is not a location*. The check is made in front of the room, in chalk, and the number was said, and the number is a permission, and the permission is nine hundred years old and off a page in a locked school store that nobody has ever read out. The new classroom has **no row at all.** Nera Oduya is in the room and **does not say her theory**, and says one professional sentence that is also the batch's last line, and the condition stands and is not met and nobody asks her. Marek says out loud that the boundary has learned how to keep a room by making it a room nobody is in, that it was cheap, that it worked, that everybody voted for it, and **that he does not know the other way and he is the man who will have to work it out** — and that **a page in a coat is not a system, that is a man, and the man is on a road.**

## Required character, relationship and power change

- **Power: none.** No bearing mark, no route change, no binding, no relay, no brace, no repeatable reading. **The Marker stage is NOT in this batch** — it is owed and scheduled at **Chapter 112, in Batch 0002**, out of the anteroom frame, and Batch 0001 must refuse it. The right hand's fine control is not recovered. The board goes from eleven entries to twelve: eleven nulls and one claim, **nine in the ordinary column and three in the unheaded one.**
- **Nera Oduya:** asked for a piece of work and not consulted for a decision; wrong in public once; **does not say her theory, and her condition is approached in a room and not met, and nobody asks her.** **No intimacy, no recognition scene, no acceleration, and no scene between her and Marek Venn.** She is not leaving and no chapter may stage her going.
- **Bel Ordry:** writes the first document in twenty-one weeks for an audience that is not in this building, is told what he is by a man with no standing who is right, does not take it back, and **is not actually doubted out loud — which is the volume's quietest beat and the one the outline most insists on.**
- **Sefu Aran:** goes up the road, **and is a boundary's absence that turns out to be a boundary's proof** — and his two true sentences are read in a room in his own building, and the promise itself is Batch 0002's.
- **Ilsa Norr:** finds the water, refuses her own school store, moves nine children across a fill, **answers the question nobody asked — that the boundary has stopped being able to be wrong in a room with children in it — and is not defending the room when she answers it**, loses a day of school, will not have it written as moved or as paid back, and writes down what she gave up.
- **Leena Orr, Pell Roke, Tams Quill, Jo Verrick, Mikka Hale, Renn Vale, Dalla Sen:** each has one job and one cost. Pell reads the count column aloud because reading numbers is what he is for. Tams explains why the crew's figure has not moved and puts **forty-five days and forty-five nights** in a column that is still unheaded. Jo finds the water with a folding rule on her knee.
- **Talla Roke is not in the building in this batch.** Her sheet is on a road and she is not a rescue and the spring is owed by nobody.
- **Iven Tallo is not asked to sign anything and notices that he was not asked.**
- **The woman from the wash rooms is not named, not asked, not rostered, and not sent for.** Her floor is not approached.
- **Oren Sill is not named.** A line refused in the fourteenth week stays refused.

## Calendar, as planned and locked forward

**The week runs Monday to Sunday. Chapters 101–105 are the twentieth week and 106–110 the twenty-first**, each chapter on the matching day of its week. **The corridor is shut on the first, second and fourth days and open on the third, fifth and sixth; the school runs on the three open days.** So the corridor is shut in 101, 102, 104, 106, 107, 109 and open in 103, 105, 108, 110 — **and Chapter 108 is a corridor-open day this batch closes.** **The spoken measure of elapsed weeks is a separate register and is not re-derived, not corrected, and not used to date anything. No day number may be attached to any in-batch event and the day-number register's highest value remains the forty-sixth day. No forward count of days or weeks on a courier, and the batch does not date a notice, a delivery or a journey.**

**Wen Pask's count is thirty-five at the end of Volume 02 and runs one a day: thirty-six through forty in Chapters 101–105 and forty-one through forty-five in Chapters 106–110. It is in the column with no heading and in nothing else, it is not a register, it may not date anything, and it may never be confused with the day-number register or remarked on as a coincidence.**

**The crew's hours do not move in this batch, and the reason is stated in a room: nobody has asked them for anything.** The boundary's hours start at a hundred and seventeen and a quarter, move, and their list must add exactly. **Tams Quill walks the board twice, names no day, and gives no reason.**

## Open questions after this batch, as planned

Where do nine children go when the wall they sit next to is the only water? Who answers for them on a fill that has moved three times and is not measured? What standing has a Compact inspector in a boundary with a charter that does not mention her office? What does a document written for one office do when a second one arrives? And why does the plan say ten in a room that is empty?

---

# Volume 02, Batch 0005 Record (retained in full; Volume 02 is closed)

## Outcome

**Volume 02, Batch 0005 is complete. Chapters 91–100 exist in `chapters/volume-02/` as finished prose in close third person on Marek Venn, with no viewpoint shift anywhere in the batch.** The batch **closes the volume**: the concrete resolution is delivered (the Hearthholds secure a legal and practical right to their essential infrastructure, and the Cartwright Guilds are **not** defeated and begin competing for control of the new network), the **volume climax is delivered and finished** (a charter is signed that keeps a route open to residents), the **Reader stage is granted here and nowhere else in Volume 02**, Nera Oduya becomes a collaborator and not a dependent, Talla Roke begins organising a courier route outside the guild system, the crew's maintenance obligation is **discharged out loud, in the room, with the reason, and not on a figure**, and the next volume's question is **asked out loud in a room and not answered.**

`outline/volume-02.md` **did not exist in this repository when this batch was written, and it did not exist for Batches 0001, 0002, 0003, 0004 or 0005.** The batch was written against the **Volume 02 section of `outline/series.md`** (*The Hearthhold Charter*, Chapters 51–100) and against `outline/ending.md`, using its central pressure, starting state, major factions/people, escalation, midpoint reversal (Batch 0003), volume climax, concrete resolution, next-volume question, and power/relationship change. Provenance is recorded in `state/current.md`. **Volume 02 closed without a volume outline, and the outline phase owed the repository a `outline/volume-02.md` reconciled against Batches 0001–0005.**

> **Update, written in the Volume 03 outline phase: that debt is paid.** `outline/volume-02.md` now exists, was authored after the volume closed, and is reconciled against all five batches in a ten-row table and ten numbered divergences. **Nothing in the paragraph above is wrong; it is a record of what was true on the day this batch was written.**

Nothing in Volume 01 is reopened. **A person is still under that lip** and the last line of the volume is that sentence. The lip, the packing, the seven terms and the eighth, the check on one note, the six watches, the eleven-foot shaft, the four treads, the ninth line, the water ledger, the wedge refusal, Form 9-C, and Oren Sill's death are all carried. **Oren Sill is not named anywhere in Chapters 91–100**, which is the correct handling and the fifth consecutive batch in which he is either unmentioned or carried only as a line refused.

## Movement

- **Chapter 91 — The Same Pail.** **The water is found by a boy who counts pails, for free, because the boundary is twelve days short of water and nobody had asked a twenty-two-year-old anything.** Wen Pask finds that the pail that comes down the middle passage is not one of the office's four, has a crust under the water line, and **the crust is lime, and lime cannot get into a pail unless it came through stone.** The objections are staged in the room's own order; Nera Oduya refuses to certify anything that is not found, tested and recorded in that order; Mikka Hale says he told her on the ninth day and not the fourth and that she is not angry. **Jo Verrick names the haunch she has touched every day for fifteen weeks.** Marek Venn gets nothing — the **tenth null** — and **Tams Quill puts it in the ordinary column and does not move it**, because this one was asked for by a person in a room and not found by the man. The chapter ends on Renn Vale saying that the outer service door is in the anteroom's rating and not in her terms and that she cannot hold both.
- **Chapter 92 — The Door Off Its Hinges.** **The cost of the water is a door, a rating, a school in a worse room, and a woman on a litter going down nine steps.** Bel Ordry asks; **Renn Vale refuses**, in four minutes, and the refusal is the price: two sets of terms on one room in two hands. The door comes off its four rusted pins and there is lime mortar and four threads of water in a dressed stone haunch that everybody has walked past for fifteen weeks. **Nera Oduya reframes a wet wall as a route** — the wall is the place the unaccounted air has been arriving — and says she had the direction of that air wrong since the collapse and refuses to say *sink* in a room where a man has been at a bar for a week. The anteroom is emptied in the pump's window. Nobody drinks anything.
- **Chapter 93 — Three Readings.** **Found, tested, recorded, in use, and not before.** Three vessels and three hours and one reading taken with the pump **off**, which is the only one of the three that is a reading. **The record is staged three times over**: the clerk reads it, **Mikka Hale strikes the word *clean*** and puts *no colour, no smell, not drunk* on all three lines in her own hand, and **Nera Oduya objects that the page is incomplete rather than wrong** and writes the hours and the pump's state on it. The clerk repeats the correction and then the strike. **The school corner's mouth is opened a hand's width for one bell: the air out of it is warm and going into the throat, and the cold current off the four unwalked treads is not the same current, and no rate is published.** The door goes back on packed with rubble, **holding and not fixed.** The pot goes to **ten** and the cook refuses to add the jars to the pot, because a sum of two things cannot be unpicked when one of them stops.
- **Chapter 94 — Where The Work Is Known.** **The third column is filled where the work is known, in four hands, in front of the room, and the fourth column is given a reason on the page instead of a heading.** The seam, the door and its rating, the school in a worse room (*it ran, and I will not have it written as though we were moved*), and the mouth — **three written *I will not*s on a page, which the air engineer says is the only thing in this Hearthhold she can be held to.** Two rows stay empty and the room does not argue, and **the anteroom's row is no longer empty and does not contain the word *person*, which the keeper read the whole row out to check.** The keeper **asks the clerk for the chalk and the clerk refuses him in front of the room**; he gives the reason to Renn Vale and not to the clerk; and **Nera Oduya strikes the word *none*** — a promise not to fill a column is a promise an office can call in — and it is replaced in her own hand. **The clerk is wrong out loud about the crew's figure and says so herself**: she had told the room on Tuesday that the water would not move it, it moved on Tuesday, it moved again on the Thursday morning, and she stands in the correction rather than being corrected. Marek Venn says *on Friday* out loud, takes it back before the count answers, and is told the count was going to let him have it.
- **Chapter 95 — The Man With The Counter.** **The guild arrives with a competent, sincere, bounded officer and a document, and nobody wins.** **Harlan Merrow**, a bond assessor and *the man who signs the fourth paragraph*, introduces himself before he is asked and asks for all of it to be entered, including the fact that the road had no date on it either. He reads the guild's six-head counter-draft, names the five heads he wrote and the one he did not, and hears three objections argued in his own favour and then the guild's real answer, which is good: **a holder is a name the office has promised not to use, and a document that cannot be sued is a document that cannot be blamed, and a thing nobody can be blamed for is a thing nobody has to fix.** Marek Venn says out loud, for the book, that it is the best case against him and that he has not got an answer. **Harlan Merrow then tells the room that the office will put the same draft to four other Hearthholds, three will accept, three will have timber, three will be able to shut a route when a clerk at a counter says so and will think they are being careful, and this boundary will be cited.** Iven Tallo and Wen Pask both refuse to sign and then **disagree in public about the order in which they said so, and the clerk writes nothing, because a man does not have to be believed twice.**
- **Chapter 96 — In Her Own Hand.** **Talla Roke's courier route is a thing she starts, in her own hand, with her own three people, and the boundary's contribution to it is a sentence and not a page.** The keeper **refuses to hold her sheet** — it is about a woman at a counter and four households, not about this boundary — and has the clerk read it out standing. The notice is *paper and small goods, no counter, no bond, no register, not asking this boundary for anything,* and it says that a paper with their mark is theirs and a paper without it came from a counter, **and that the boy is not on it and is not coming.** The boundary sends **two sentences with no name and no signature**: the road up the middle passage is not shut against the people who live behind it and will not be shut for a delivery, **and at the bottom of those four sets of steps there is nothing.** Wen Pask asks out loud whether the route carries people; the sheet says no, not this month, not as a favour, not to a boy. Iven Tallo says the hard thing — **you will be paid and you will not be a carpenter, and both are true, and being kept is not being trained** — and **the wage goes into the column with no heading, the first wage ever in it, and Tams Quill still refuses to head it**, for a new and worse reason: a wage that can be pointed at is a wage that can be stopped, and the man who stops it will not be the man who is owed.
- **Chapter 97 — The Charter.** **The volume climax. It is staged exactly the way the release was staged: in a room, with a person who will not sign it, and with every objection written out loud before anybody picks up the chalk.** Butcher's paper, the cook's hand, an office's number at the top and **no name anywhere**, because *a charter with a name at the top of it is a charter with a holder.* Eight clauses are argued line by line: the keeper rewrites clause four to *when the office asks, in a room, to a person* and takes back the promise to show; **the keeper rewrites clause two against himself so that it binds the office and not only the boundary; the air engineer strikes the woman's covers out of the reason clause because nobody asked her; the cook strikes the second gate out of the charter because it is not a thing to hand a kitchen's own water to a stranger in a document about a door**, leaving the sheet and the page to disagree by one number on purpose. Clause nine says the ninth room, the four treads, the wash rooms, the mouth and the blank line are **not in it, and that is deliberate.** **Iven Tallo will not sign**, in four words, and is not argued with, and gives the real reason later on a bench. **Marek Venn refuses in nine seconds**, because a signature by a man who is leaving is a name against a route. **Harlan Merrow says the office can neither grant nor refuse it**, that a boundary instrument needs nothing from them, that what the office does with it is receive it, file it and cite it — and that clause four is the whole of what the boundary has given away, and is worth more than they think and less than they want. Seven signatures. **The charter keeps a route open to residents and puts no holder against the route in a register kept by the office, and the reason why not is written in the document.**
- **Chapter 98 — What She Is Asked.** **Nera Oduya is asked for a piece of work instead of consulted for a decision, and the room does without her visibly for four hours and says so.** She arranges it, and enters that she arranged it, and states the price: the room now knows it does not need to ask, and the day it stops asking is the day there is a real question about the air. Bel Ordry asks her to write what she thinks is most likely to go wrong in the anteroom, and it is the **eighth line of the third column** and the only one on the page that is a refusal to write something: the rating depends on a word, and *a rating that depends on a word is a rating that a tired person at the fourth bell in a bad month will say the word to.* **She is at the clinic return from the first bell and is asked at the fifth bell, after the six things have been read and after she is back in the room; the two are never in the same bell and the chapter does not put her in two places at once.** The keeper rules the third way — **the word gets said to a page and not to a door** — and enters the price of holding a page, which is that every refusal anybody hands him becomes a line in a thing he carries. The air board goes **onto the page and not into a coat**, because a board in a hand is an office and there is exactly one road up this passage. **The thirteenth line stays unwritten** and the reason is in her own hand in the third column: the column it wants is the column for an owner, and a person is not an owner, and anybody who wants to know what the air is can come to the valve at the second bell and hold the release themselves.
- **Chapter 99 — One Room.** **The Reader stage, arrived out of the plan and the charter, and it is a stage and not a solution.** With the plan out of the coat in four parts because a stranger is coming to look at it, Marek Venn puts his left hand on the fourth row without deciding to, and **instead of nothing, a place: the anteroom.** The cost is immediate and named: the left hand clamps and shakes, the headache comes behind the left eye with a floor to it, and the anteroom's count arrives as a **sound** under the check on the pump and cannot be separated from it. **He says it in a room as a fact before he has decided anything, and that is the failure mode the stage is priced at** — Nera Oduya makes him say how he knows, he says *because the fourth row is empty*, she says that is a hole in a document and not a reason, and he cannot say it twice. **Tams Quill enters it as a claim and not a reading**, with the condition that it may not be built on until somebody can do it again; it is **the eleventh**, in the ordinary column, in Marek's own hand, and the count does not move it. He tries again at the third bell and gets nothing, and the count follows him up the nine steps and is still there at the seventh bell in a room he is not in, and the clerk enters that she does not know whether that is a fault. **At the sixth bell the clerk takes the brass survey rule out of the coat's pocket because his right hand cannot open the pocket, and it lies on the table saying nothing, and it has said nothing for ten weeks, and nobody looks at it.** The anteroom then runs its own full drill and **Jo Verrick finds a finger and a bit out of the head of the frame**, because clay shrinks as it dries; the air engineer's rule is the right rule and the wrong room's rule, and the clerk enters both side by side. Marek Venn is not asked into the shoring and is not in it, and says the whole of it out loud: **it did not save anybody, it was worth an afternoon, his left hand closed properly this morning and does not close tonight, and it was one room, and there are eight others on that page and he does not know one of them, and anybody who comes and asks him to read a boundary is going to be told no.**
- **Chapter 100 — Not On A Figure.** **The guilds begin competing, the four houses are answered out loud, the obligation is discharged on a sentence, the next volume's question is asked and refused an answer, and a person is still under that lip.** A **halfpenny sheet** comes up the passage — the Cartwrights' maintenance undertaking, offered at the middle gate's store, timber in the month after next, the fourth line verbatim — with Nell Ferrin's true sentence on the back, that the office printed a hundred and counted them, that the office is doing it because the first one said no, and that she has watched a thing like this twice and both times it worked. Tams Quill walks the board twice and **names no day**: the crew at **fifty-two and a half**, the boundary at **a hundred and seventeen and a quarter**, and the fourteen-item list adds exactly; **every one of the crew's four hours is a door or a page, the water's record and its three readings are in the boundary's column with the cook's name and the clerk's on them, and so the crew's hours did not go up for the water, because a good week is not a work.** **The keeper refuses to use his own condition.** The pot stopped falling on the third day of the eighteenth week and has been ten days for twelve days, the condition was met in the strictest reading, and **a condition you can satisfy is not a condition, it is a date with a number on it**, and his was satisfied by luck, and it stays where it is.
